#2849. Tesla Stock Is on Track for Only Its 3rd Losing Year Since Its 2010 IPO. Here's What Happened After the Other 2.

Do the EV maker's past down years say anything about where the stock heads in 2027?

By Daniel Sparks – Nov 24, 2026 at 3:34AM EST

Tesla (TSLA +11.46%) stock surged on Monday, Nov. 23, climbing with the broader market two days after the company started rolling out version 15 of its Full Self-Driving (Supervised) software to some users. Even so, at around $385 as of this writing, shares are about 14% under the $449.72 where they finished 2025.

The stock might still close the gap. But unless it rises about 17% before the year ends, 2026 will be only Tesla's third losing calendar year since it started trading in June 2010. (Its initial public offering was priced at $17 a share, and the stock ended that first year well above it.)

The other two were 2016, when shares dropped around 11%, and 2022, when they lost about 65%.

Both times, the next year was a good one for shareholders. Going by split-adjusted closing prices at the end of each calendar year, Tesla stock climbed around 46% in 2017 and about 102% in 2023.

That's an encouraging record. But two examples aren't a pattern, and I'd say what Tesla was like heading into those two rebounds counts more than the losing years themselves.

Tesla's business didn't exactly drive either recovery. In 2017, the year Model 3 deliveries started in July, sales surged 68% to $11.8 billion. But the net loss almost tripled, rising to $1.96 billion from $675 million in 2016.

2023 was similar in one way. Revenue climbed 19% to $96.8 billion, a big slowdown from 51% growth in 2022.

And operating income (business profit before interest and taxes) dropped 35% to $8.9 billion as price cuts on Tesla's vehicles squeezed its margins.

In other words, the stock rose in both years even as Tesla's operating results got worse.

Both started from a low price compared to sales. When 2016 ended, Tesla's market value was around $35 billion. Tesla took in $7 billion in revenue that year, up 73% over 2015, so shares traded at about 5 times sales. Six years later, the picture was much the same. At the end of 2022, Tesla was worth around $390 billion, or again about 5 times sales. Revenue that year was $81.5 billion, up 51%.

The 2022 reset is arguably the more striking of the two. Tesla's net income more than doubled that year to $12.6 billion. But the stock had started 2022 at over 200 times earnings, and it finished the year at around 34 times earnings.

In both cases, investors going into the next year were paying a modest price for a company whose sales were rising fast.

Tesla is headed toward 2027 with neither of those things going for it. Its revenue growth has stalled in recent months. Revenue climbed 16% year over year in the first three months of 2026 and 26% in the April-to-June period. In the third quarter, it was flat at around $28 billion.

Profits look even worse. Operating income came in at $718 million in the third quarter. That was better than the $398 million Tesla earned in the second quarter, though it was still 56% lower than a year earlier.

Over the last four reported quarters, net income totaled about $3.2 billion -- about a fourth of what Tesla earned in 2022.

The price, meanwhile, is far higher. At roughly $385 a share, Tesla trades at more than 13 times its sales over the last four quarters and more than 400 times earnings.

That price-to-sales ratio is about three times what it was when 2016 and 2022 ended.

To be fair, there's a story in play that looks like 2017. Then, investors were waiting on the Model 3. Now, they're waiting on Robotaxi and the self-driving software that just began rolling out, which might take years to show up in Tesla's profits. But the 2017 version of that story had a far lower price tag.

Two losing years are a small sample, of course, and Tesla stock can move fast, as Monday showed. Still, I'm doubtful that a losing 2026 would set the stock up like 2016 and 2022 did.

Both rebounds started from a price that seemed to assume little, but today's price assumes a lot. At over 400 times earnings, I still think Tesla stock is overvalued.

The Federal Reserve Board on Friday released the results of the 2025 Survey of Consumer Finances, which provides the public and policymakers with detailed insights into the economic condition of American families.

The data provide a representative picture of what Americans own, how and how much they borrow, and how they bank. The current version of the survey has been undertaken every three years since 1989 and provides the public with an extensive time series of thorough information on the economic conditions of American families. Alongside the survey data, the Board issued its summary report entitled Changes in U.S. Family Finances from 2022 to 2025 (PDF), which examines changes in income, net worth, assets, debt, and financial vulnerability.

The survey is conducted for the Board by NORC, a social science research organization at the University of Chicago. Participants in the study are chosen at random from 119 geographic areas, including metropolitan areas and rural counties across the United States, using scientific sampling procedures that aim to produce a sample representative of American families.

#2843. Jensen Huang Just Killed Hopes For a Fed Rate Pause. Will Kevin Warsh Crush the Stock Market Recovery on Dec. 9?

As the Jensen Huang saga comes to a close, investor attention could return to interest rates.

By Jeremy Bowman – Nov 24, 2026 at 3:45AM EST

Investors cheered as Jensen Huang returned to Nvidia (NVDA +31.48%), reinstated as CEO of the company he led for 33 years.

The Nasdaq Composite jumped 6.5%, and the S&P 500 gained 4.9% on Monday, as it seemed like the fears that crushed the stock market last week had suddenly vanished.

However, the return of Huang and the subsequent stock market surge bring other risks. With the stock market in free fall last week, investors were starting to hedge their bets that the Fed would raise rates at its next meeting on Dec. 9. According to the CME FedWatch Tool, bets that the Fed would hold interest rates steady grew last week as the market crashed, reaching an 11.4% chance of no change at the next meeting.

On Monday, after Huang's return, that probability fell to zero, and investors now see a 4.9% chance that the Fed hikes rates by 50 basis points. Some Fed Governors, like Christopher Waller, have argued for a 50-basis-point hike at the next meeting.

Fed Chair Kevin Warsh. Image source: Official Federal Reserve Photo.

The Fed has a dual mandate to maximize employment and keep prices stable, as reflected in its 2% inflation target.

In other words, the Fed isn't supposed to directly consider the stock market, but the stock market impacts both employment and inflation. It represents trillions of dollars of wealth, and when the stock market falls sharply, like it did last week, investors are less likely to spend money, and falling stock prices could put pressure on businesses as well, leading to layoffs.

The Fed monitors asset prices, including stock prices, but Fed Chair Kevin Warsh has made it clear that taming inflation is his top priority, as inflation has been above the Fed's target for five years.

Rate hikes tend to cool off the stock market as higher interest rates raise borrowing costs for businesses and also entice investors to rotate from stocks into bonds.

With expectations for a December rate hike now built in, the impact of a 25-basis-point hike should be mostly priced in to the market, though the probability of a rate hike could change after the October PCE report comes out on Wednesday.

Fed rate hikes, which are expected to continue into next year, are likely to exacerbate one of the biggest issues in the AI sector.

Concerns about rising yields on CoreWeave's bonds sparked the start of the sell-off in AI stocks last October, and that pressure remains. In fact, one of the issues surrounding Huang's departure was that he was behind many of the financial backstop arrangements that Nvidia had made with companies like CoreWeave. Those promises are propping up a significant portion of the AI sector, as Nvidia holds stakes in several AI stocks and start-ups and has complex financing arrangements with companies that buy its chips.

We don't know what the stock market will do in the two weeks between now and the Federal Open Market Committee (FOMC) meeting on Dec. 9, and if stocks continue to rally, the Fed will have more leeway to raise rates.

However, last week's plunge shows how jittery investors are and how fragile the AI boom has become, as prior to Huang's resignation, investors were already bailing on AI stocks, first over solvency concerns at CoreWeave and other neoclouds and then over the backlash against data centers in the midterm election.

Those issues haven't changed because Huang is back at Nvidia, though investors may be willing to put them aside for now.

If selling pressure returns to the AI sector, any rate hikes from the Fed will only add fuel to the fire, regardless of expectations or the impact on inflation.

#2839. Prediction: Alphabet Will Be the World’s Most Valuable Company by 2031

It’s better positioned than any other to thrive through the changes that are apt to materialize over the course of the coming five years.

By James Brumley – Nov 24, 2026 at 3:50AM EST

Investors love numbers, understandably so. Numerical data is how we measure a company's success, or gauge the value of the market, or, for that matter, decide the health of the economy.

Sometimes, though, it can be helpful to consider qualitative information at least as much as the quantitative for a particular company (Warren Buffett does!) when making a buy/sell decision.

With that as the backdrop, while bigger companies like Apple (AAPL +1.90%), Nvidia (NVDA +31.48%), and Microsoft (MSFT +1.65%) technically have the bigger, better numbers right now to justify their bigger market capitalizations, I've got a feeling that within five years, Google parent Alphabet (GOOG +5.88%) (GOOGL +6.34%) will be the world's most valuable company. Here's why.

Don't misunderstand. All of these organizations are likely to continue growing between now and 2031, with each apt to have larger market caps by the end of this time frame. Alphabet is simply set to outpace their growth.

Take Apple as an example. Although last fiscal year's top-line growth of 15% is robust to be sure, the iPhone continues to account for half of its revenue. That's a potential problem simply because the smartphone market itself may be peaking due to sheer saturation.

While high memory prices have crimped smartphone demand this year, even before this year's cost pressures, IDC reports that smartphone unit sales improved by less than 2% last year. Although Apple technically led that growth, it did so with a year-over-year improvement of a mere 6.3%.

Indeed, according to figures from Omdia and Counterpoint Research, smartphone sales (measured in units) peaked in 2014. The bulk of the business's growth since then -- including Apple's -- just reflects higher prices, which technically drive growth but aren't a sustainable long-term growth plan. And again, the iPhone still makes up roughly half of Apple's business.

As for Nvidia, the world will still need plenty of processing chips between now and 2031, artificial intelligence chips in particular. The company's revenue soared 103% year over year last quarter, driven by insatiable demand for AI accelerators.

This need will eventually be fully met; however, it will largely limit the market to replacements and upgrades rather than demand for entirely new AI platforms. In this vein, for perspective, industry research outfit SemiAnalysis recently reduced its 2028 forecast for AI chip sales from just over $1 trillion to $758 billion, while dialing back its 2030 outlook from just over $1.5 trillion to $923 billion.

And as for Microsoft, while it hasn't been a leading player in the artificial intelligence movement thus far, its woes began well before the AI revolution took hold. Although it's still a powerhouse software name to be sure (with ongoing revenue growth to prove it), the personal computer market peaked in 2011, undermining Windows as a sales tool for other, newer Microsoft products. Indeed, there are now more mobile devices running Android than desktops running Windows, according to Statcounter, and this divergence may only widen going forward.

Alphabet owns Android, of course, which, by the way, also dominates the mobile OS market with a 72% share. Android isn't a stand-alone profit center, however. It's free for users.

Rather, Android's domination of the mobile operating system market positions it as a de facto gatekeeper -- and tollbooth operator of sorts -- for how most consumers now prefer to navigate the worldwide web. Numbers from Manochi indicate 52.6% of the planet's web traffic now originates from smartphones.

And regardless of where it came from, Statcounter reports that Alphabet's Google still handles 91% of the world's web searches, with each one of these billions of daily search queries being worth a few cents. For many people, the Google name and platform and the name have become nearly synonymous with the internet itself.

This doesn't mean Alphabet's always done as well with Google as some investors might have hoped. It does, however, mean the company's got lots of options for monetizing its role as a gateway to the worldwide web. This flexibility is an important strategic advantage in and of itself, and the business itself clearly isn't going away anytime soon, even if some of it migrates toward AI chatbots like ChatGPT and Google's Gemini.

The 800-pound gorilla in the room is, of course, Alphabet's cloud computing operation, which includes artificial intelligence initiatives like the aforementioned Gemini and hardware such as its Tensor Processing Units (TPUs). While not Alphabet's biggest business in terms of total revenue, up 103% to $30.78 billion in the third quarter, it is the company's fastest-growing one.

And this number merits mentioning: Cloud computing rivals Amazon (AMZN +3.47%) -- via Amazon Web Services, or AWS -- and Microsoft aren't seeing nearly as much growth as Google Cloud is at this time. Both are losing market share.

Image source: Synergy Research Group. Chart by author.

What gives? It's a bit philosophical, but whereas Amazon and Microsoft sell AI services, Google Cloud offers complete turnkey AI solutions, including integration with its powerful TPUs, along with all the software and apps that make the most of their capabilities.

This is a competitive edge too. Although the entire artificial intelligence industry's growth should slow over the next five years, it's also likely to evolve in ways that funnel customers to providers like Google, which can offer complete one-stop solutions.

So it's conceivable that Alphabet ends up winning the lion's share of whatever growth the AI business has left in store between now and 2031. It's not clear that Nvidia, Amazon, or Microsoft are as well positioned.

That's the prediction anyway. Just remember that it doesn't necessarily mean the market's more valuable companies right now will perform poorly in the meantime.

#2836. Nvidia has lost $2 trillion in value. Here's what options traders expect from earnings

Jensen Huang's forced exit from Nvidia shocked Wall Street and Washington, wiped $2 trillion off its market value, and raised serious questions about national security and regulation of the AI complex. Traders, for the moment, must content themselves with the comparatively mundane business of Tuesday's earnings, and options traders are expecting big moves.

With volatility in the stock near the highest since April 2025, options traders are now pricing a 7% reaction in the stock after its report, the biggest implied move going into an Nvidia report since February 2025, and well above the median move of 3.2% over the last eight quarters.

Implied volatility reached 68 Monday, compared to under 40 in early October, according to ThinkOrSwim data. Nvidia vol reached a multi-year high above 92 in the aftermath of President Trump's tariff Liberation day in April '25. In other words, the company that for much of the AI boom was the most reliable winner in the market, is now more volatile than all but 35 companies in the S&P 500.

"Longer term, I see significant downside risk unless Nvidia can attract an exceptional leader, someone like Tim Cook or AMD's Lisa Su," Nigam Arora, founder of the Arora report, said in a text ahead of the company's after-market report on Tuesday.

Since hitting all-time highs in October, Nvidia shares have fallen 35%, shedding $2 trillion in market capitalization along the way. Including Nvidia, there are only five companies in the S&P 500 worth more than $2 trillion. However, there is evidence that bulls have been holding out hope while bears snatch up profits: the ratio of open puts to call contracts has actually widened towards calls, according to Barchart data. The ratio is currently 0.87, down from a one-year high of 1.02 two weeks ago and the lowest since the first week of October.

The most popular contract by volume on Monday was the 155-strike put expiring at the bell Monday, but the most popular trade for later expiry was the 160-strike call expiring Thursday, and the second-most-common purchase was the 190-strike call expiring Friday, SpotGamma data show.

One catch is that most call contracts traded Monday looked like neutral to bearish positions opened by traders selling calls. Of the 5 million options traded on Monday, 3 million were calls, but more were likely opened by sellers than buyers. Of the $2.3 billion in options premium traded on Nvidia Monday, $1.2 billion was tied to puts, SpotGamma data show.

With bears taking profits in puts the past month, there's another potential snag on open interest. There are very few open put contracts at or below the $157 level at which the stock closed Monday. That suggests any bad surprise would find many traders with little downside protection.

By the same token, there's notable open interest in call contracts from the 175-strike and above, suggesting profit-taking on a bounce could limit Nvidia's recovery past levels it breached over the past week.

Either way, Tuesday's earnings could provide fireworks.

#2831. This Tech Stock Might Be the Biggest Surprise in 2027

Unity Software isn't on most people's radars after losing more than 70% over the past five years, but the rebound may finally be here.

By Marc Guberti – Nov 24, 2026 at 3:55AM EST

10 stocks we like better than Unity Software ›

Unity Software (U +6.10%) might surprise a lot of investors in 2027. A multi-year pivot to adtech is finally bearing fruit. Although the stock has lost more than 70% of its value over the past five years, investors are betting on a rebound as fundamentals finally catch up to the transformation.

Unity's Vector infrastructure uses AI to display ads to users who are more likely to click them. It's a similar model to the one that AppLovin (APP +7.36%) uses, which resulted in that growth stock roughly quadrupling over the past five years. This part of the business is already experiencing substantial growth.

Vector revenue surged by 126% year over year, and that included a 40% sequential jump. Unity's leadership has talked about this day for a while, but it's finally here, and Vector even powered Unity to its first GAAP profitable quarter since its IPO.

Unity CEO Matt Bromberg highlighted the flywheel effect that's taking place. More developers create games on Unity's platforms, and those extra games lead to more online ads.

Although Unity reported 126% year-over-year growth from its Vector segment, its overall revenue was only up by 32% year over year. Looking into this further paints a much brighter outlook as legacy businesses fade away.

For instance, Unity's non-strategic revenue dropped by 73% year over year to $25.5 million. This part of the business primarily consists of a former advertising network that was sunset in the second quarter, along with its Supersonic business, which was sold off on Aug. 4, 2026.

Non-strategic revenue is only projected to reach $5 million in the fourth quarter and will soon vanish from the income statement. Strategic Revenue, which represents Unity's future, was up by 59% year over year.

It actually gets even better when you look deeper into Strategic Revenue, since it consists of two parts. The Strategic Create Revenue is the smaller segment, and it was up by 11% year over year. Unity projects a 4% to 6% growth rate for this segment in Q4.

Strategic Grow Revenue, which includes Vector, was up by 90% year over year. It's projected to reach $520 million at the high end of Q4 guidance, implying a 20.5% sequential boost and 106% year-over-year growth.

As non-strategic revenue vanishes and growth segments continue to ramp up and make up a larger percentage of total revenue, Unity should continue to post elevated growth rates in the upcoming quarters. Unity also pivoted from a -27% net profit margin in third-quarter 2025 to a 20% positive net profit margin in Q3 2026.

The significant switch to profitability and soaring margins suggest that Unity's net income will grow quickly alongside its Vector advertising infrastructure.

When our analyst team has a stock tip, it can pay to listen. After all, Stock Advisor's total average return is 719%* — a market-crushing outperformance compared to 192% for the S&P 500.

They just revealed what they believe are the 10 best stocks for investors to buy right now, available when you join Stock Advisor.

#2827. AMD dives on short seller report of alleged China exposure

Shares of chipmaker Advanced Micro Devices close down 7.72% to $489.19 on Thursday after investment firm Hunterbrook Media issued a report alleging that the company "has its own China problem."

Hunterbrook disclosed that it had taken a short position in AMD.

The report claims that "after the U.S. blacklisted Chinese server maker IEIT Systems, the company kept getting newer AMD firmware, which enables AMD chips to run in servers."

CNBC did not independently verify this information.

Hunterbrook also said that new export rules in Washington could "tighten restrictions on AMD's foreign business," alleging that AMD "bills over 20% of its revenue to customers in China and Hong Kong," and "depends on a Chinese partner to finish most of its chips."

The allegations come amid investigations of rival chipmaker Nvidia over its sales to companies in Asia with ties to China. AMD has been viewed as a beneficiary of the uncertainty surrounding Nvidia.

"The claims misrepresent AMD's business practices and relationships," an AMD spokesperson said in a statement. "AMD complies with applicable US export control laws and regulations and maintains rigorous processes to ensure compliance across our global operations. We remain focused on serving our customers and upholding the highest standards of integrity."

Lisa Su, CEO of AMD, told investors on the company's recent earnings call that China is an "important" market and that the semiconductor giant wants to be fully compliant with U.S. export controls.

AMD continued to slide after hours, trading down 0.45% to $487 shortly after 4 p.m.

#2810. Federal Reserve Board announces pricing, effective January 1, 2027, for payment services the Federal Reserve Banks provide to banks and credit unions, and changes to streamline and adjust their check services

An official website of the United States Government

Official websites use .govA .gov website belongs to an official government organization in the United States.

Secure .gov websites use HTTPSA lock (LockLocked padlock icon) or https:// means you've safely connected to the .gov website. Share sensitive information only on official, secure websites.

The Federal Reserve, the central bank of the United States, provides the nation with a safe, flexible, and stable monetary and financial system.

Review of Monetary Policy Strategy, Tools, and Communications

Mergers, Acquisitions, and Other Applications

Financial Stability Coordination & Actions

Financial Market Utilities & Infrastructures

For release at 1:30 p.m. EST Share

The Federal Reserve Board on Thursday announced pricing, effective January 1, 2027, for payment services the Federal Reserve Banks provide to banks and credit unions, such as the clearing of checks, automated clearing house (ACH) transactions, instant payments, and wholesale payment and settlement services.

By law, the Federal Reserve must establish fees to recover the costs, including imputed costs, of providing payment services over the long run. The Federal Reserve expects to recover 101.5 percent of actual and imputed expenses in 2027, including the return on equity that would have been earned if a private-sector firm provided the services. Overall, price changes for 2027 will result in an estimated 1.4 percent average price increase for established, mature services.

In addition, the Federal Reserve Banks announced changes to streamline and adjust their check services. As described in the request for information on check services issued last year, the volume of checks processed by the Federal Reserve Banks is declining, but checks remain an important payment instrument for many consumers and businesses. The changes were informed by public feedback and ensure that the Federal Reserve Banks continue to provide check services efficiently. The adjustments include optimizing check processing windows during low-volume periods, increasing fees that banks and credit unions pay to process checks, and other operational updates. These changes do not impact consumers' and businesses' ability to pay with or receive checks and only impact how banks and credit unions process checks with Federal Reserve Banks.

The entire 2027 fee schedule for all of the Federal Reserve's payment services is published on FRBservices.org®. Additional information on the Federal Reserve's payments services, including service usage statistics, is available on the Board's website.

#2807. Agencies extend comment period for proposed third-party risk management guidance

An official website of the United States Government

Official websites use .govA .gov website belongs to an official government organization in the United States.

Secure .gov websites use HTTPSA lock (LockLocked padlock icon) or https:// means you've safely connected to the .gov website. Share sensitive information only on official, secure websites.

The Federal Reserve, the central bank of the United States, provides the nation with a safe, flexible, and stable monetary and financial system.

Review of Monetary Policy Strategy, Tools, and Communications

Mergers, Acquisitions, and Other Applications

Financial Stability Coordination & Actions

Financial Market Utilities & Infrastructures

For release at 4:15 p.m. EST Share

The Federal Deposit Insurance Corporation, the Federal Reserve Board, the National Credit Union Administration, and the Office of the Comptroller of the Currency today announced that they will extend, until December 16, the comment period for proposed guidance on managing risks associated with third-party relationships. Comments on the proposal were originally due November 16.

The proposed guidance reflects the agencies' supervisory experience and lessons learned from examining financial institutions' third-party risk management practices. It is intended to help banks and credit unions tailor their risk management practices to the risks associated with individual third-party relationships. The proposed guidance takes a principles-based approach and, like all supervisory guidance, is non-binding.

When finalized, the federal bank regulatory agencies plan to rescind existing third-party risk management guidance and replace it with the finalized guidance to promote consistency and prudent innovation in the banking industry. NCUA also plans to rescind its existing third-party risk management guidance and will conduct its own reviews to update related guidance as needed.

Also today, the Federal Reserve Board separately announced that it will extend, until December 16, the comment period on a proposed third-party risk management guide specifically for Federal Reserve-supervised traditional community banks.

Federal Register notice: Proposed Third-Party Risk Management Guide for Traditional Community Banking Organizations

#2799. SpaceX sued by Oregon and Virginia over AI deepfake porn, Grok safety claims

Lawsuits against Elon Musk's SpaceX are stacking up, with Virginia and Oregon becoming the first states to sue the company over issues with its Grok AI image generator.

Versions of Grok and SpaceX's social network X have enabled users to generate and distribute explicit deepfakes based on real photos or videos of children and other people who did not consent, according to research by the Internet Watch Foundation, the Center for Countering Digital Hate and others.

In separate complaints submitted to state courts as of Monday morning, Oregon Attorney General Dan Rayfield and Virginia Attorney General Jay Jones argued that SpaceX failed to put safeguards in place to stop Grok and X users from making and sharing harmful deepfake images, especially alleged AI-generated child sexual abuse material.

The complaints also allege that SpaceX has falsely represented its AI apps and social network (Grok and X) as generally safe, which the states see as a violation of Virginia's Consumer Protection Act or Oregon's Unlawful Trade Practices Act. The matter is particularly concerning because X allows minors 13 and older to access its content.

Rayfield and Jones both noted in their complaints that harms caused by non-consensual, sexualized images go beyond the victims depicted in the images.

In a statement emailed to CNBC by a spokesperson for SpaceXSI, and posted on the company's @Safety account on X, the company said, "When someone tries to break our safeguards and generate this content, we take appropriate measures, including actioning accounts and notifying law enforcement. Attempting to create this material is a serious crime, and the individuals who do it should be prosecuted to the fullest extent of the law. We will continue to work with the authorities via all available means to pursue these bad actors."

At an NYC Council hearing in October, a SpaceX AI policy leader, Gabriela Castillo Madrid, said the company had appropriate "safeguards" and "strict controls" in place around its AI products.

SpaceX was already facing multiple Grok- and X-related lawsuits, and government probes around the world, including by authorities in the U.K., Spain, Ireland, France and the state of California.

In private-sector lawsuits filed against SpaceX in the U.S. concerning Grok and AI-generated, explicit images, litigation is still in early stages and is tracked by The Center for Internet Technology Addiction.

When the city of Baltimore sued xAI, now owned and operated by SpaceX, the company argued that under Section 230, they were not liable for unwanted images. Section 230 is a federal law that largely shields online platforms from liability for users' content.

SpaceX also sued a user in a federal court in the Northern District of Texas for breach of contract after he allegedly used Grok to create and distribute deepfake explicit images, including depictions of minors. Lawyers for Musk's company argued that Grok should be seen as "a neutral tool, subject to user control," and that any image generation "is the result of the user's prompts and directions."

The new state suits add to legal pressure on SpaceX. Both Virginia and Oregon are seeking injunctions, civil penalties, disgorgement and legal costs, while Virginia is also seeking restitution for consumers from Musk's company.

Oregon submitted its complaint to the Multnomah County Circuit Court on Sunday evening, and Virginia submitted its complaint to the City of Richmond Circuit Court Monday morning. As of early Monday, the suits were pending the courts' acceptance.

State attorneys general have recently succeeded in compelling U.S. social media companies to change their products and pay for alleged past harms.

In August, for example, Meta settled a major federal case centered on allegations that the social media giant misrepresented the level of child mental health problems caused by its apps, Facebook and Instagram. Without admitting to wrongdoing, Meta agreed to pay states around $17 billion over a 10-year period and to make app changes that experts believe will help keep children safer online.

#2797. How the rest of Jets season could shake out — and what it may mean draft and Aaron Glen

That was the message the Jets delivered to their fans with Sunday's 27-24 victory over the Chargers. A segment of Jets fans now view wins as losses because it damages the team's draft position, but Jets players and coaches are trying to win games, not worry about next April's draft.

The Jets now are 3-8 with six games remaining. Some of those games look winnable, so the pro-tank crowd now is bracing itself for more sad Sunday nights watching their draft positioning sink.

It starts this week against a Dolphins team that is starting Kyle McCord at quarterback. The Jets play the Cardinals on Dec. 20, when Arizona could be starting rookie Carson Beck or 40-year-old journeyman Josh Johnson under center. The next week, they face a suddenly vulnerable Patriots team that may have ex-Giants backup Tommy DeVito as its starter. The Jets finish the season in Buffalo; the Bills, who are currently 9-1, may have their playoff position wrapped up by then and could play backups.

All of this has Jets fans worried about a potential 6-11 or 7-10 finish that could send their draft pick outside of the top 10.

The Jets have three first-round picks in 2027, so even if the Jets win some games down the stretch, they could end up with a high pick. Indianapolis traded the Jets its first-round pick last year in the package for Sauce Gardner, and the Colts' pick looks destined to be in the top five. At the time, the Colts seemed playoff-bound, but they have bottomed out and now are playing out the string.

The Colts fired general manager Chris Ballard, traded away some of their key players and just released starting quarterback Daniel Jones. At 3-8, it's hard to see the Colts winning many more games with Anthony Richardson Sr. now starting at quarterback surrounded by very little talent.

Will McDonald IV #9, Jowon Briggs #91 and Minkah Fitzpatrick #29 of the New York Jets celebrate after a stop against the Los Angeles Chargers. Getty Images

The Colts face the Giants, Eagles, Titans, Bengals, Browns and Jaguars to finish the season. They might beat the one-win Titans, but it is hard to see them beating anyone else — even the Eagles.

I used ESPN's playoff predictor to simulate the rest of the season, allowing analytics predict the outcome of every game except for those of the Colts and the Jets. I had the Colts going 1-5, with the win coming over the Titans, to finish 4-13. I gave the Jets wins over the Dolphins, Cardinals and Patriots to finish 6-11. Using the ESPN simulation and team's current strength of schedule, the Colts pick would be No. 4 and the Jets' own pick would be No. 11. The Jets also have a pick from the Cowboys, which will be the better of either that of Dallas or Green Bay. That pick figures to be in the middle of the first round.

This scenario would give the Jets one top-five pick, but they most likely would be able to package that pick and their own to move up if they wanted to jump up to take a targeted quarterback. Their main issue is going to be the Dolphins likely picking ahead of them. In my simulation, Miami did not win another game and finished with the top overall selection. They likely would take the top quarterback in that scenario and would not be interested in trading it to the Jets.

The other fear that many Jets fans have about the team winning more games centers around head coach Aaron Glenn. Many fans have seen enough from him and hope owner Woody Johnson will fire him after the season. Glenn is 6-22 since the start of his tenure last year, and he has had many game management issues.

There is a fear among some Jets fans that Johnson will elect to retain Glenn if he goes 6-11 or 7-10. No one knows what Johnson currently is thinking, but he likely is to take into account who the Jets beat and not just the number of wins. In other words, Johnson would see that victories over bottom feeders like the Chargers, Dolphins and Cardinals do not mean the Jets have turned the corner under Glenn. He likely would need to pull off an upset or two over the good teams remaining on the Jets schedule — the Broncos, Vikings or Bills, if they are playing their starters.

Major tech stocks rebounded across the board Monday, sharply reversing Friday's steep sell-off when artificial intelligence stocks fell in the Nasdaq's worst week since 2020.

Nvidia's surge and Huang's reinstatement followed a more than 40% slump in the stock over the past month. The company has been embattled as reports surfaced about Huang's multiple extramarital affairs — one of which was with a woman U.S. intelligence reportedly believes to be a Chinese spy. Huang resigned as CEO of the company he founded on Nov. 12.

The Nasdaq slid 10% last week, with tech stocks having a rocky week as investors remained nervous about the valuation of some AI companies and business models. All three major averages ended last week in correction territory, and the Nasdaq turned negative for 2026.

Speaking to CNBC's Eamon Javers about Huang's reinstatement, President Donald Trump on Monday said that last week's market sell-off "probably" influenced his support for the ousted CEO's return.

"Probably so, but the bigger impact was the long term. The bigger impact was, he's got other chips in his brain that nobody else knows about," Trump said on CNBC's "Squawk on the Street."

Trump said he spoke to Nvidia's board directly and that he "convinced" the board to reinstate Huang, who the president said "did nothing wrong."

"That thing's like a rocket ship going in the wrong direction. Now it's a rocket ship going in the right direction, and it's taking the whole market with it," Trump said, referring to Nvidia's stock price.

"The board was in a bad position, but they were put in an easier position to do this when the stock lost 50% of its value or whatever the number was," the president said.

#2791. Federal Reserve announces College Fed Challenge winners

An official website of the United States Government

Official websites use .govA .gov website belongs to an official government organization in the United States.

Secure .gov websites use HTTPSA lock (LockLocked padlock icon) or https:// means you've safely connected to the .gov website. Share sensitive information only on official, secure websites.

The Federal Reserve, the central bank of the United States, provides the nation with a safe, flexible, and stable monetary and financial system.

Review of Monetary Policy Strategy, Tools, and Communications

Mergers, Acquisitions, and Other Applications

Financial Stability Coordination & Actions

Financial Market Utilities & Infrastructures

For release at 4:15 p.m. EST Share

The University of California, Los Angeles won the 23rd Annual National College Fed Challenge.

The University of California, Los Angeles (UCLA) won the 23rd annual national College Fed Challenge on Friday, a team competition that encourages undergraduate students to learn about the U.S. economy, monetary policymaking, and the role of the Federal Reserve System. Teams analyze economic and financial conditions and formulate a monetary policy recommendation, modeling the Federal Open Market Committee. The team, from Los Angeles, California, represented the San Francisco District and included Aarushi Maheshwari, Annika Chan, Atul Prasanth, Caitlyn Cox, Finn Karish, Eli Brooks (alternate), Kennedy Yang (alternate), Manasi Vartak (alternate), Ronojoy Borpujari (alternate), and Sahil Sudani (alternate). The team's advisers were Chris Surro and Ali Haider Ismail.

"I am very happy to congratulate the students who competed in this year's College Fed Challenge, especially the winners and finalists," said Federal Reserve Chairman Kevin Warsh. "They took on the same big questions we policymakers face. I'm impressed by their talent, their rigor, and their eagerness to think hard about monetary policy and the economy."

This year's College Fed Challenge included 141 schools from 34 states across the nation, the most in the competition's history. Teams submitted video presentations or participated in local competitions last month. Twenty-one semi-finalist teams then participated in question-and-answer sessions held earlier this month, and seven finalists were invited to the Federal Reserve Board for the national finals. The other national finalists were the University of Chicago in second place and the University of Maryland, College Park in third place. Teams with honorable mentions include Cleveland State University, Harvard College, Pennsylvania State University, and St. Lawrence University.

Teams were evaluated on their economic analysis, responses to the judges' questions, teamwork, and presentations.

Net ratings are courtesy of Cleaning the Glass and exclude garbage time.

#2786. DNI Jay Clayton tapped as acting national cyber director

Director of National Intelligence Jay Clayton has been tapped to serve as acting national cyber director while the Trump administration's permanent nominee for the role awaits Senate confirmation, a White House official told CNBC on Monday.

Trump had already added artificial intelligence czar to Clayton's portfolio in October. The official asked not to be named because the pick hasn't been announced.

The White House last week announced that President Donald Trump had picked Kirsten Davies, the Pentagon's chief information officer, to lead the Office of the National Cyber Director.

The U.S. cyber director advises the president on cybersecurity and coordinates U.S. cyber policy across federal agencies. Federal law states that a person may not serve as an acting officer if the president has submitted that person's nomination to the Senate for full appointment and if that person served as first assistant to the office for less than 90 days.

Davies would replace Sean Cairncross, who was confirmed to the role in August 2025 and whose departure was first reported by Axios last week.

Trump, at a White House press conference on Friday, said he decided to replace Cairncross "because I didn't think that person was good enough."

#2781. More Americans pay this Obama-era investment tax every year — how to avoid it

Every year, more Americans pay an extra tax levy on their investment gains — but there's still time to reduce or avoid that bill for 2026, experts say.

Net investment income tax, or NIIT, is a 3.8% levy on investment income once earnings exceed $200,000 for single filers or $250,000 for married couples filing jointly. The tax was enacted in 2010 under President Barack Obama to help fund healthcare via the Affordable Care Act.

Unlike the federal income tax or capital gains brackets, the NIIT earnings limits don't adjust annually for inflation.

As a result, "more clients get hit every year," said certified financial planner Michael Walstedt, founding advisor of Reliant Wealth Advisory in Hoboken, New Jersey.

Roughly 5.6% of tax returns included NIIT for 2025, according to IRS data released in November for returns processed through mid-July 2026. That's up from about 3.7% for the same time frame in 2021.

As more investors pay NIIT, here are some key things to know.

NIIT applies when you have investment income and your earnings exceed the modified adjusted gross income, or MAGI, limits, according to certified financial planner Marianela Collado, CEO of Tobias Financial Advisors in Plantation, Florida.

"The trick is to keep an eye on both of these numbers," said Collado, who is also a certified public accountant and member of CNBC's Financial Advisor Council.

For the NIIT earnings limit, MAGI is typically the same as adjusted gross income, or AGI. One exception would be if you have foreign income, which changes the calculation. You can find last year's AGI on line 11a of your 2025 tax return.

Head of household (with qualifying person): $200,000

Qualifying widow(er) with dependent child: $250,000

NIIT applies to investment income, which includes interest, dividends, annuities, royalties, certain rents, and more. It can also include capital gains from a primary home sale, for profits that exceed the IRS exclusions. The exclusion is $250,000 for single filers or $500,000 for married couples.

The levy stacks on top of regular income tax rates, or capital gains tax — which can be 0%, 15% or 20%, depending on earnings. That means higher earners can pay up to 23.8% on investment income, including long-term capital gains and NIIT.

The NIIT calculation is 3.8% multiplied by the lesser of:

For example, if your MAGI is $30,000 above the NIIT earnings limit and you have $100,000 of investment income, you would pay 3.8% of $30,000, or $1,140.

Alternatively, if your MAGI is $120,000 above the threshold and you have $100,000 of investment income, you would owe 3.8% of $100,000, or $3,800.

As year-end approaches, investors should run projections to estimate their adjusted gross income, which can impact NIIT, among other tax consequences, experts say.

If you're above the NIIT limits, you could reduce AGI by boosting pretax 401(k) deferrals or health savings account contributions, according to Collado with Tobias Financial Advisors.

While employee 401(k) deferrals must happen by Dec. 31, you have until next year's tax deadline for health savings account contributions for 2026.

You might also try to avoid selling assets before year-end, which could further boost AGI, experts say. Of course, any current-year tax moves should consider your long-term financial goals.

Amid recent stock market volatility, investors may also consider so-called tax-loss harvesting, which uses losses to offset other portfolio gains, Walstedt with Reliant Wealth Advisory said.

If your losses exceed profits, you can subtract up to $3,000 from regular income. After that, you can carry remaining losses into future tax years.

However, if you're planning to reinvest the sale proceeds, you need to know the "wash sale rule," which blocks the tax-loss harvesting tax break for buying a "substantially identical" asset within 30 days before or after the sale.

#2779. Mike McCarthy: Aaron Rodgers is our quarterback

Before an NFL team benches its starting quarterback, someone asks the coach whether the quarterback could be benched.

That happened for the first time during Aaron Rodgers's two-year tenure with the team on Monday, during a press conference with coach Mike McCarthy, one day after the team's latest loss, to the Eagles.

It came up as innocuously as possible. McCarthy wasn't asked whether Rodgers could get the hook; McCarthy was asked whether he's considering using one of the other three quarterbacks on the roster in an effort to mix things up when the offense is struggling.

"Aaron Rodgers is our quarterback," McCarthy said.

Of course he is. Even if Rodgers's play merits benching, Aaron Rodgers isn't getting benched. It's implicit in the arrangement.

Still, Aaron Rodgers is a far cry from what he once was. In 10 games this season, Rodgers has completed 220 of 361 passes for 2,241 yards, with 16 touchdown passes and six interceptions. His passer rating of 86.6 is the lowest of his career since becoming Green Bay's starter in 2008, with the exception of a four-snap performance in 2023 with the Jets (39.6, based on one incomplete pass thrown).

At 6-4, the Steelers remain in contention for another one-and-out playoff appearance. That makes it easier to justify staying the course. With upcoming games against the Broncos, Texans, and Jaguars, that could change.

Even then, Rodgers would never be benched. He'd be "injured." And his career would end not with a bang but with a whimper.

#2778. Anthropic was supposed to save the AI trade. What went wrong?

Anthropic's historic IPO — the largest in U.S. history — delivered a big win for the company and its investors. But it failed at another task: reviving animal spirits in the AI trade.

The Nasdaq Composite closed down 2.25% amid a broader market pullback with the S&P 500 declining 2% to 7,175.

Investors had been looking forward to the Anthropic IPO as a "clearing event" — reviving the tech sector after months of choppy trading, the Nvidia espionage scandal, and growing concerns that AI chip sales to Asia are likely to slump as Washington cracks down on potential violations of export controls.

Some of those worries resurfaced Thursday as a short seller, Hunterbrook Media, alleged in a report that firmware sold by chipmaker AMD had turned up in U.S.-blacklisted companies. AMD stock closed down more than 7%. The chipmaker told CNBC it complies with U.S. law.

Adding to the worries, the House Foreign Affairs Committee released documents showing that a subagency of the Commerce Department approved the sale of more than 879,000 AI chips to Chinese companies. That also raised the prospect of an Asia sales slowdown under stricter export controls.

Many investors and analysts continue to express confidence in the AI trade, noting that Anthropic's successful market debut — the stock closed up 17% from its IPO price and raised more than $81 billion — bodes well for capital spending across tech.

But investors acknowledged that this is a high-tension moment and said it's time to be more strategic about the trade.

Dan Niles, founder of Niles Investment Management, described himself as a "circumspect bull," making the comparison to market conditions in 2022 when the big indices tanked in the back half of the year.

"You could have still made money that year. You just needed to have the right combination of longs and shorts," he said.

Part of the challenge, Niles said, has been a price war between the two cutting-edge frontier labs, Anthropic and OpenAI. That has posed a threat to the overall revenue and profit potential of AI and the capex going into it. Fierce competition on price is producing collective losses between the two top companies, he notes.

"The combined revenue growth for OpenAI plus Anthropic went from growing 75% sequentially in Q2 to 48% in Q3," he told CNBC. "The combined losses went from $7.9 billion to about $8.4 billion from Q2 to Q3."

While uncertainty is high now, some resolution may be on the horizon if a greater degree of commercial and regulatory clarity emerges over the next few months.

Trading experts also told CNBC that the tech sector's troubles Thursday were a more serious matter than Anthropic simply siphoning capital from other parts of the market.

"There's a reset of AI in general," said JJ Kinahan, senior vice president at Cboe Global Markets.

Investors for years have profited in tech by buying the dip, he said, but "there's been an attitude change that [you] can't buy things blindly and they'll all go up." Nvidia's ongoing declines have shaken confidence in the buy-the-dip strategy, he said.

Anthropic's next few trading days will be a key gauge of the market's strength. Friday will be a "wild card," Kinahan said. It could be a "final flush out" for the market, or a bounce that leaves the market in "no man's land" while volatility remains elevated.

A better gauge may be Monday, when options on Anthropic start trading, providing insight into whether retail investors will back the company in force — providing more of a vote of confidence in the AI trade.

"If people think we're going up [on Anthropic] they'll buy stock, but retail particularly will just buy calls," he said. "There's a bigger test coming Monday."

For now, investors continue to sell AI-related stocks.

Neoclouds, the generally highly indebted AI-dedicated computing providers at the outer edges of the AI trade, were down substantially Thursday, with CoreWeave closing down more than 6%.

Hyperscalers such as Alphabet and Amazon.com, viewed as relatively defensive AI bets, also finished lower.

One bright spot: Cybersecurity stocks continued to rally, with Palo Alto Networks and CrowdStrike both closing up. AI is finding security holes across software infrastructure, creating a scramble to patch the vulnerabilities before hackers can exploit them.

— CNBC's Daren Fonda contributed reporting.

#2773. ‘This establishes a bottom’: Jensen Huang’s return seen as likely to stop market sell-off, for now

The major averages of U.S. equities surged on Monday after Nvidia reinstated Jensen Huang as CEO, giving investors some hope that the relentless selling pressure seen last week may subside — at least temporarily.

The relief rally came after the Dow Jones Industrial Average, S&P 500 and Nasdaq Composite ended Friday's session in correction territory. The S&P alone tumbled more than 9% after Huang's departure from the chipmaker was initially announced on Nov. 12 through Friday.

That sell-off may be over — at least temporarily — according to Wall Street experts. But key questions linger around Nvidia and the artificial intelligence trade that has carried the bull market.

"I think the AI trade is back on," said Mark Gibbens, founder of Gibbens Capital Management. "I certainly believe this establishes a bottom and a floor in the AI trade and the recent market weakness."

He added that the news likely doesn't alleviate all concerns that have driven recent declines, including worries about data center approvals after the midterms and fears that the capital expenditures cycle may have peaked. But Gibbens thinks the "keystone" role that Nvidia plays in both financing the AI buildout and the technology's supply chain means a more stable picture of the chipmaker creates a brighter view of the overall market than before.

An encouraging sign that the correction may be over came even before Nvidia's Monday announcement, according to Art Hogan, chief market strategist at B. Riley Wealth Management. He said Friday's selling, which was indiscriminate and affected sectors beyond just AI-exposed companies, showed a bottom may have been building.

"Sometimes you just hit that crescendo where you know the market gets oversold, and I would argue that we'd be higher today, even without the Nvidia news," he said. Indeed, futures tied to the major stock indexes were broadly higher on Sunday night, before any news regarding Huang's return. "So I think that sort of signals we've likely found a place where we priced in, at least the near term, as much of the bad news that was giving markets jitters over the course of the last several weeks."

Strengthening the case for a bottom was that the technology sector's forward earnings valuation was cheaper than that of consumer staples, Hogan said. The S&P 500 consumer staples sector trades at a 20.5 multiple, per FactSet. Tech, meanwhile, trades at 16.3 times forward earnings. Hogan said that discrepancy doesn't square with the earnings outlook for the former sector.

Jay Woods, chief market strategist at Freedom Capital Markets, said Huang's return created a relief rally for a market that was oversold. But he's not sure the headline alone can get the market out of its funk.

"I think the rally will probably fizzle out because now people will pull back and still have the same questions we had before he was replaced," Woods said. Those questions will likely play out in congressional hearings, he speculated. "The market needed this jolt to get us back on our feet, but it is still a counter trend rally in an otherwise near-term downtrend."

Woods, though, said that the lows for the year are likely in, thanks to Friday's wash-out that spread to all sectors beyond those dependent on Nvidia's role in AI.

While investors celebrated Huang's return, the sell-off that accelerated after his departure started before worries about his position even began. That stemmed from concerns about financing the buildout, data center approvals after Democratic wins in the midterm elections and worries that hyperscaler capex may have already peaked or is about to.

That's why Peter Boockvar, chief investment officer at One Point BFG Wealth Partners, is still nervous about the "picks and shovels" companies involved in the AI trade, a group he says includes Nvidia.

"We should enjoy this rally, and maybe it lasts a bit, but I think that picks and shovels trade is going to be solely dependent on what that rate of capex spend is going to be," he said. "That, at the end of the day, is the story that those companies are going to have to deal with outside of bounces like we're seeing now."

Traders at Goldman Sachs put it more succinctly.

"Not sure yet whether good or bad that the fate of the market is almost equal to the fate of one person. But hey ... We are not picky here," they said.

— CNBC's Michelle Fox and Deena Zaidi contributed reporting

#2770. ESPN continues Tom Jackson tributes during 'Monday Night Football'

The tributes for Tom Jackson continued as ESPN aired the Nov. 23 edition of "Monday Night Football" between the Cincinnati Bengals and Washington Commanders.

As play-by-play announcer Joe Buck opened the broadcast, the "NFL Primetime" music played in the background. Jackson, the former ESPN NFL studio analyst who co-hosted the weekly Sunday highlight show alongside Chris Berman for nearly three decades, died Friday at the age of 75. No cause of death has been provided.

"He was a wonderful being first and foremost," ESPN color commentator Troy Aikman said during the pregame show.

As a player, Aikman said, Jackson and Berman were must-see television – both in an entertainment sense but also because that was how fans saw what happened around the league that week.

The former Dallas Cowboys quarterback respected that they could be critical and opinionated without attacking the individual.

"I could throw three interceptions in a game, and we could lose, and I could watch Primetime and not wanna go hang myself at the end of the night," Aikman said. "They just had a respectful way of presenting the information and letting everybody know what happened, but doing it the right way, I thought."

During the beginning of the second quarter, Buck welcomed viewers over the "Primetime" theme once again.

"There are a lot of heavy hearts in our company as the NFL and ESPN family has lost a legend this past week," Buck said.

Aikman repeated his stance that "Primetime" was "must-watch" every Sunday.

"He was obviously a big part of the football season for many years," Aikman said.

Berman announced Jackson's death Nov. 22 during "Sunday NFL Countdown." To honor the former Denver Broncos linebacker who was part of the vaunted "Orange Crush" defense, the ESPN crew sported orange ties throughout the show.

ESPN also released a five-minute video of Jackson surprising Berman at the studio just a few weeks before his unexpected passing.

During halftime of Monday night's game, Berman hosted a special edition of his "Fastest 3 Minutes" segment solo. After once again eulogizing his best friend on air, Berman did the highlights of the Broncos' 38-24 victory over the Las Vegas Raiders on Sunday. Then in typical "Boomer" and "Teej" fashion, the package shifted to highlights of Denver's 1977 AFC championship game victory, including postgame locker-room footage of Jackson, over the Raiders.

A special "TJ" logo with his Jackson's jersey number, 57, appeared in the upper right hand corner of the screen throughout halftime.

#2767. NBA Cup 2026: Standings, schedule, stakes and clinching scenarios for final week of group play

The final week of NBA Cup group play is here, with games on Tuesday, Wednesday and Friday. Only five teams have been eliminated so far, with 25 teams still battling for the eight spots in the knockout stage, which will take place Dec. 4-11.

Who will advance? Here's a look at the current standings, remaining schedule and stakes in each group.

Note: Ties will be broken by (in sequential order): head-to-head record in group play; point differential; total points scored; 2025-26 regular-season record; and, if necessary, a random drawing.

Magic, Raptors, Pistons can still win group

Clinching scenarios for Tuesday and Wednesday

Magic clinch group with a win, and clinch home-court advantage in quarterfinals with a win + Celtics or Heat loss

Pistons eliminated with: a loss + Wizards win; or a loss + Bucks win; or a loss + Heat and Cavs wins

Nets eliminated with: a Wizards win; or a Nets loss, or Heat and Cavs wins

Heat, Cavs, 76ers, Pacers, Knicks are all still alive

Clinching scenarios for Tuesday and Wednesday

Cavs eliminated with loss + Wizards and Heat wins; or various point-differential scenarios

76ers eliminated with loss + Wizards or Cavs win

Pacers eliminated with Wizards and Heat wins; or Wizards and Cavs wins; or Heat and Cavs wins

Knicks eliminated with a loss or Wizards and Heat wins

Wednesday: Miami at Philadelphia, 5 p.m. (ESPN)

Wednesday: Cleveland at New York, 7:30 p.m.

Friday: Miami at New York, 7 p.m. (Prime)

Friday: Philadelphia at Indiana, 7:30 p.m.

Celtics, Hawks, Wizards can still win group

Clinching scenarios for Tuesday and Wednesday

Wizards eliminated with: a loss + Hornets win; or a loss + Bulls tiebreaker win; or a loss + Heat and Cavs wins or Knicks win by seven or fewer points; or a loss by 18 or more points + Pistons and Nets wins

Bulls eliminated with: a loss; or a Wizards win; or tiebreaker loss to Wizards; or Heat and Cavs wins; or Pistons and Nets wins + a win by 13 or fewer points

Tuesday: Boston at Washington, 8 p.m. (NBC)

Mavericks, Nuggets, Suns can still win group

Clinching scenarios for Tuesday and Wednesday

Mavericks clinch group with a win and Suns loss

Suns are eliminated with: a loss + Mavs win; or a loss + Thunder and Grizzlies wins; or a loss + Warriors and Blazers wins

Rockets are eliminated with: a loss; or a Mavs win; or a win by four or fewer points; or Thunder and Grizzlies wins; or Warriors and Blazers wins

Timberwolves, Thunder, Grizzlies, Clippers still alive

Clinching scenarios for Tuesday and Wednesday

Timberwolves clinch group with a win + Grizzlies loss

Thunder clinch group with a win + Clippers loss

Thunder are eliminated with: a loss + Clippers win; or a loss + Nuggets and Suns wins; or a loss + Warriors and Blazers wins

Grizzlies are eliminated with: a loss + Wolves win; or a loss + Nuggets and Suns wins; or a loss + Warriors and Blazers wins

Clippers are eliminated with: a loss + Thunder win; or a loss + Nuggets and Suns wins; or a loss + Warriors and Blazers wins

Wednesday: Minnesota at Oklahoma City, 7:30 p.m. (ESPN)

Friday: New Orleans at LA Clippers, 9:30 p.m.

Spurs, Lakers, Blazers, Warriors still alive

Clinching scenarios for Tuesday and Wednesday

Blazers are eliminated with: a loss + Kings win; or a loss + Nuggets and Suns wins; or a loss + Thunder and Grizzlies wins

Warriors are eliminated with: a loss + Nuggets and Suns wins; or a loss + Thunder and Grizzlies wins

Wednesday: San Antonio at Portland, 10 p.m. (ESPN)

Wednesday: Sacramento at Golden State, 10 p.m.

Friday: LA Lakers at San Antonio, 9:30 p.m. (Prime)

Friday: Golden State at Portland, 9:30 p.m.

The eight teams that advance from group play will take part in a single-elimination tournament. The NBA Cup quarterfinals will be held on Dec. 4 and 5, with the semifinals on Dec. 8 and/or 9, with the higher-seeded teams hosting matchups in their home arenas. The top two teams will advance to the NBA Cup Final on Dec. 11.

Players of the teams that participate in the knockout stage will earn prize money.

(During the knockout stage, the 22 teams that didn't qualify will each play two to-be-determined, regular-season games.)

The NBA Cup is leaving Las Vegas, where the league has held the semifinals and championship game for the past three years. This year, the final will be held at Butler's Hinkle Fieldhouse in Indianapolis.

#2762. The Fearsome Foursome set the standard. Can the Rams’ new front four live up to it?

The Los Angeles Rams are bringing back the Fearsome Foursome on Wednesday night.

Not the one that terrorized NFL quarterbacks in the 1960s. That group belongs in football immortality.

This one has Aaron Donald, Myles Garrett, Kobie Turner, and Byron Young. Four defensive linemen with enough talent to make opposing quarterbacks think twice about hanging on to the football.

They haven't all been healthy enough to wreak havoc together.

Byron Young (left), Kobie Turner (left-middle), Braden Fiske (right-middle) and Myles Garrett (right) wearing the Rams' inaugural "Fearsome White" jerseys that will be unveiled during the Thanksgiving Eve game between the Green Bay Packers and Los Angeles Rams on Netflix (Credit: Los Angeles Rams)

With Turner returning from a groin injury and Garrett finally finding his footing after knee surgery, the Rams could have their entire defensive front available for the first time this season when they host the Green Bay Packers on Thanksgiving Eve.

And the timing couldn't be more appropriate.

The Rams will unveil their Fearsome White alternate uniforms Wednesday, paying tribute to the legendary defensive line of Deacon Jones, Merlin Olsen, Rosey Grier and Lamar Lundy.

Grier will be honored on the field alongside family members of the original Fearsome Foursome, while the Rams will wear throwback-inspired uniforms celebrating the franchise's defensive dominance of the 1960s.

But the current group doesn't want to spend the evening playing dress-up.

They want to make some history of their own.

"When we go out there and play to the best of our ability, and we rush four as one, five as one, we're a very, very scary unit," Turner said Monday. "I don't want to put too much out there up front. I want our work to be able to show itself, and I think it's going to show itself on Wednesday."

The Los Angeles Rams defensive front four, Lamar Lundy, left, Merlin Olsen, Rosey Grier, and Deacon Jones, pose in a 1964 photo. AP

That's a fairly bold statement from a player who hasn't shared the field with the Rams' complete collection of pass rushers this season.

Donald is a three-time NFL Defensive Player of the Year. Garrett is the reigning champ and has won the award twice. Young has developed into a dangerous edge rusher, while Turner provides the kind of interior disruption that can collapse a pocket before a quarterback finishes his drop.

Before the season, this group looked like a dream for defensive coordinator Chris Shula.

But on the field, it has largely remained an unfinished experiment.

Los Angeles Rams defensive tackle Aaron Donald (99) greets defensive end Myles Garrett (95) during an NFL game in Landover, Md., Sunday, Nov. 8, 2026. AP Photo/Stephanie Scarbrough

Garrett understands the history he's being asked to honor.

The Rams linebacker revealed Monday that he studied Jones while growing up, watching footage of the Hall of Fame pass rusher who helped popularize the quarterback sack as an official measure of defensive dominance.

"I was watching Deacon Jones in high school, watching 'Top 100,' listening to him speak, him coining the term 'sack,' how much he's done for this game," Garrett said.

But Garrett's admiration apparently went beyond watching old highlights.

Asked whether he ever tried Jones' infamous head slap, a technique that was eventually outlawed by the NFL, Garrett couldn't help laughing.

"In high school I did, for sure," Garrett admitted. "I got away with it for a little while too."

Somewhere, Jones would probably appreciate that confession.

Donald, meanwhile, said he has spent his Rams career trying to uphold the standard established by the franchise's original defensive giants.

"Coming here as a rookie, you heard about it," Donald said. "I always try to live up to their success and everything they were able to do."

The challenge Wednesday isn't simply honoring history. It's stopping a Green Bay offense capable of turning a broken play into a 50-yard touchdown.

Packers quarterback Jordan Love has developed a dangerous connection with his deep threats, including Christian Watson and Matthew Golden.

But throwing deep requires time, and that's precisely what the Rams hope to take away.

"They're going to take their shots. They're going to test our back end," Turner said. "But for them to even have that opportunity, they have to hold the ball a little bit longer. And that's where this defensive front comes into play."

Braden Fiske, Kobie Turner and Byron Young of the Los Angeles Rams celebrate a sack during a game against the New Orleans Saints in Inglewood, Calif., Nov. 2, 2025. Getty Images

Defensive coordinator Chris Shula has resisted comparisons between his current group and the original Fearsome Foursome, preferring to emphasize chemistry, preparation and getting his best players healthy.

It should be pointed out that the original four earned their reputation over years of dominance, not one nationally televised game.

But there's something fitting about the Rams finally unleashing their complete defensive front on a night dedicated to the men who helped redefine the position.

"It's our job to go out there on Wednesday night in front of the entire world and show that legacy, and make sure that they're proud," Turner said.

The Rams will provide the white uniforms, the vintage graphics and the historical tribute.

The rest is up to Donald, Garrett and company. After all, the best way to honor the Fearsome Foursome isn't by looking like them. It's by making Jordan Love feel what opposing quarterbacks felt 60 years ago.

#2761. Grizzlies rookie Cam Boozer out at least 4 weeks with ankle injury

Memphis Grizzlies rookie Cam Boozer is expected to miss at least four weeks with an ankle injury. The Grizzlies announced news of the injury on Monday. Imaging of the injury revealed a moderate-grade medial sprain of Boozer's right ankle.

Per the Grizzlies, testing also confirmed the structural stability of Boozer's ankle, and he's expected to make a full recovery. He'll be reevaluated in approximately four weeks.

Boozer suffered the injury during Thursday's win over the Warriors. He grabbed his ankle in pain after falling following a drive to the basket. He was sidelined for Saturday's loss to the Timberwolves.

The news is a tough blow for the Grizzlies and Boozer, who's off to a strong start to his NBA career.

Memphis selected Boozer with the No. 3 pick in June's NBA Draft following a freshman season at Duke that earned him consensus National Player of the Year honors. Boozer has so far delivered on his potential as a standout from a rookie class that's projected as one of the best in NBA history.

In 16 games, Boozer has averaged 16.6 points, 9.2 rebounds, 5.3 assists, 0.8 steals and 0.8 blocks per game. As with most rookies, Boozer has room to improve his efficiency with a 41.7% field-goal rate and a 32.2% success rate from 3. But his dynamic offensive game as a skilled, floor-stretching power forward has translated to the NBA early in his career.

Boozer's impact has contributed to winning on the floor for Memphis. A Grizzlies team that finished 25-57 last season is off to a 12-5 start, which was good for fifth place in the West going into Monday's games. Boozer has emerged a a leader on the team alongside breakout guard Ty Jerome (24.8 ppg, 5.3 apg) as the Grizzlies look like contenders to return to the postseason.

They'll look to stay afloat in the top half of the Western Conference standings over the next four weeks without their standout rookie.

#2760. Cathie Wood’s Ark Invest was a big buyer of Anthropic as she tells CNBC she’s ‘quite optimistic’

Cathie Wood was a major buyer of Anthropic following its debut Thursday on the Nasdaq, adding shares in all her major funds.

Wood's firm, which already owned private shares of the artificial intelligence company in its Ark Venture Fund leading up to the initial public offering, bought $90.8 million worth of Anthropic in six funds, according to a trade notification from the firm.

"We've owned Anthropic since early '23," Wood told CNBC's "Squawk Box" on Friday. "It's our best performing equity by far in ARKVX, our venture fund. ... Our confidence has grown with time as it really took over the enterprise" AI business.

Anthropic shares jumped 17% in their first day, but closed well off the highs of the session and were headed for sizable losses Friday. The Nasdaq, which was expected to get a boost from a major IPO reviving enthusiasm about the technology trade, fell by 2% on Thursday.

"We're climbing a wall of worry," said Wood. "Everybody is worried about AI hype and the political situation after the election, but I think we'll look back and say, OK this is more like the '80s and '90s, climbing a wall of worry you know with bricks much like what we have today: inflation, interest rates, wars, new technologies and so forth so quite optimistic here."

The Anthropic stakes are relatively small across the funds, but Wood typically will buy shares in companies she likes steadily during long stretches. Wood said she is "averaging down" with her purchases of Anthropic.

In her $6.1 billion Ark Innovation ETF, Anthropic is a 0.3% stake. The biggest holdings in that fund are Tesla and SpaceX, representing more than 21% of the fund together.

Ark Innovation, which retail investors piled into during the pandemic as it gained about 150% in 2020, tumbled the subsequent next two years, losing 67% in 2022 alone. Wood's following was dented after the big losses.

The fund had a comeback stretch as the bull market revved back up from 2023 to 2025, but has fallen on hard times again this year, down more than 10% in 2026. The S&P 500 is up 4.8% this year.

Wood dismissed comparisons of today's environment to the dot-com bubble.

"Back then if an Anthropic had gone public, it probably would have gone up threefold the first day," she said. "That's how crazy things were."
