#2722. Blazers shoot the lights out as Embiid-less Sixers fall to 14-4

We haven't seen them yet this year — do all Western Conference teams just never miss their three-pointers?

The Sixers were blown out by the Trail Blazers 128-111 Monday as Portland made 45% of their threes on the night.

After missing his last two games, Jaylen Brown was soaring until he ran into foul trouble, finishing with 20 points on 8-of-14 shooting along with two rebounds and four assists. LeBron James had his best shooting night as a Sixer, going 8-of-13 from the floor to finish with a season-high 23 points along with seven rebounds and three assists.

Tyrese Maxey had 22 points and five assists on 7-of-16 shooting. VJ Edgecombe finished with 12 points, seven rebounds and seven assists shooting 5-of-11 from the field. Deni Avdija led all scorers with 31.

Joel Embiid (illness) was the only Sixer out tonight. Ja Morant and Shaedon Sharp were out for Portland, both out with long-term injuries.

The Sixers got Brown involved with two early touches. He drew a foul on the first and got an open jumper on the second thanks to a healthy stiff arm to Avdija. Jabari Walker found Edgecombe by the basket as the Blazers were trying to get down the floor, capping off a 9-2 Philly run to start the game and prompt an early timeout for the Blazers.

That timeout got Avdija going offensively. After beating Brown off the dribble he nailed a couple of threes. LeBron was creating threes on the other end as well. A pumpfake of his sent Avdija flying by, allowing James to drill an open one from the corner. He drove baseline on the next possession and found Brown, also wide open in the corner.

Coming off the bench, Dominick Barlow was the first able to stop Avdija, swatting away a layup attempt. The Sixers didn't come out with a poor effort defensively. They were able to lock up Damian Lillard for a sho-tclock violation, but Portland opened the game hot from deep, making four of their first nine threes of the game.

Adem Bona also returned after missing time with a neck injury. He was able to quickly grab an offensive rebound upon entering the game and when the ball got swung to him, he was able to put it on the deck and get to the basket for a dunk. He even hit a bit of a move to get by Jrue Holiday. He was relieved by Walker after only a couple of minutes. Walker picked up a missed Dean Wade three and put it back to keep the Sixers up by two after the first.

With some more starters back on the floor the offense started to hum again. Brown and Edgecombe each drilled pull-up jumpers. LeBron threw a pair of nice passes to Walker who converted on both of them, and got a skyhook for himsel.

Defensively, Walker was a pretty small look against Donovan Clingan. The Sixers tried to sit in some zone to combat that, but quickly went back to man. On top of those paint concerns, the Blazers remained pretty hot from three. They hit two more to take a lead of their own, despite LeBron hitting a big three that got the crowd off it's feet in between them.

Brown did not look like he had missed the last two games at all. He just looked so smooth getting to his spots in the midrange. He put up 19 in the first half on 10 shots. The Sixers needed every bit of it because they had less answers for Avdija on the other end.

After starting the game with Brown on Avdija, the Sixers tried Dean Wade before ultimately landing on Barlow to close the half. Barlow did foul him but Avdija missed both free throws. On the next trip down Barlow was able to force a bad airball that happened to fall to LeBron who threw a gorgeous fullcourt pass to Brown for the fast break dunk. After that tied the game, Lillard was able to draw a foul on Edgecombe shooting a three-pointer. Maxey found LeBron in the corner on the ensuing possession for another Sixers' buzzer-beater, keeping the game tied at the half.

LeBron tried to throw another fullcourt bounce pass to give Maxey a fast break layup, but Sidy Cissoko got his hands on it to break it up. They continued to move the ball well though as Edgecombe got open in the dunker for James to find him, before dishing to a cutting Walker who drew a foul. Both teams struggled from the line to start the second half as Walker split his pair. Avdija was unable to convert an and-1 and Clingan gave the fans a five-count of chicken nuggets very early.

The Sixers also had to deal with another extended period of Brown on the bench after he picked up his fourth foul. James seemed to still be up to carry the scoring load, drilling his fourth three of the night on his fourth attempt.

For as good as LeBron was, the Sixers struggled to supplement him offensively with Brown sitting in foul trouble. They had a six-minute stretch only scoring one field goal. Maxey tried to get more aggressive but had a pull-up three attempt blocked by Robert Williams III. Lucky for him the Blazers weren't able to secure the rebound and Maxey was able to scoot to the basket for a reverse layup.

Any minor blip for the Sixers offense was notable because they just could not get stops on the other end. As they sat in a zone Portland continued to drill everything from deep. After Scoot Henderson drifted into a gap to drill a three, Holiday hit one in Wade's face on the following possession. On the other end Barlow was desperately trying to create a hustle bucket, but he came up a bit gingerly after going for a rebound and getting his shot blocked by Cissoko. Jayson Kent drilled one from the corner to give the Blazers a nine-point lead as the third came to a close.

Henderson and Kent drilled a couple more threes to start the quarter, extending Portland's lead to its largest of the night. Nick Nurse tried to let it ride as LeBron got to the basket, but after Edgecombe dribbled the ball off his foot out of bounds and the team surrendered a layup off an offensive rebound, Nurse called an angry timeout less than two minutes into the quarter.

Edgecombe was able to run out off a loose ball and get a transition layup and the Blazers immediately called a timeout to squash a potential run. The Sixers had chances coming out of the timeout, forcing a couple turnovers, but they couldn't take advantage on the offensive end. Lillard hit a pull-up which got the Blazers offense rolling again and the Sixers had no response.

Portland's height advantage had begun to wear the Sixers down as well as they started surrendering more. offensive rebounds. No matter what center option the Sixers tried, Portland's center would have an inch or two in height advantage, there was just nothing to be done about that, save for some Tacko time. Avdija stole an inbounds pass of off Maxey as Sixers fans started to flock to the exits with over five minutes remaining.

#2721. Six key questions raised by Jensen Huang's return to Nvidia

Jensen Huang made a dramatic return as Nvidia CEO on Monday, less than a week after board Chairman Harvey Jones said directors did not intend to reinstate him. The board later voted unanimously to bring Huang back.

Nvidia shares surged as much as 36% during the session and closed 31% higher.

The reversal followed a steep decline in Nvidia shares, which closed at $125.53 on Friday even after the company reported strong third-quarter results. A petition calling for Huang's return had drawn more than 200,000 signatures, according to its organizers.

The rally spread across the broader market, with the Nasdaq Composite gaining 6.5% and the S&P 500 rising 4.7%, each posting its strongest session since April 2025.

Nvidia's board said it had received input from senior U.S. government officials and that its investigation into Huang's conduct would continue.

Still, the board's reversal, President Donald Trump's administration's role and continued scrutiny of Nvidia's China business leave major questions for both investors and lawmakers.

Sen. Jack Reed, a member of the Senate Banking Committee, urged Treasury Secretary Scott Bessent and Commerce Secretary Howard Lutnick on Monday to appear before the committee to discuss the case.

Reed did not lay out a specific list of questions for the two officials, but his statement cited reporting that Trump had been briefed by intelligence officials led by then-National Security Adviser Marco Rubio before intervening in support of Huang's return.

Reed's call adds to mounting pressure on both Nvidia and the Trump administration to explain what they knew and when.

Rep. Gabe Amo, D-R.I., vice ranking member of the House Foreign Affairs Committee, said Huang’s reinstatement could broaden congressional scrutiny rather than bring it to a close.

“I think it frankly broadens the scope of the query,” Amo told CNBC, adding that lawmakers should also examine administration officials involved in decisions affecting Nvidia and chip sales to China.

Amo said he expects the planned closed-door interview with Huang on Dec. 1 to proceed and that Congress would continue seeking answers both privately and publicly in the coming months.

“He should start purchasing his plane tickets to Washington,” he said. “It is abundantly clear that this is ripe for questioning by Congress.”

On Nvidia's third-quarter earnings call on Nov. 17, Jones said the board had accepted Huang's resignation and did not intend to reinstate him.

Less than a week later, the board reversed course, saying Monday that it had unanimously voted to bring Huang back after discussions with him and input from senior U.S. government officials.

Trump said he had spoken with board members and that his administration provided assurances based on the information available to it. But the board has not publicly detailed what it was told.

"The board decided to do what we wanted to do," Trump told CNBC, adding that he believed his intervention had been important.

"I've been speaking to the board directly and indirectly, and I felt very strongly about it," he said.

Trump also called into an Nvidia board meeting to argue for Huang's return, Semafor reported, citing people familiar with the matter.

Reed has called on Huang to disclose his communications with Trump and on Nvidia to release a transcript of the board's call with the president.

Lawmakers are still seeking answers about the alleged smuggling of Nvidia products to China, their possible diversion for military use and possible advancement of Chinese AI capabilities.

The Senate Banking Committee has scheduled Huang to testify on U.S. export controls on Dec. 3. House lawmakers have also confirmed closed-door interviews with Huang on Dec. 1 and Nvidia General Counsel Tim Teter on Dec. 2.

"The Nvidia board's brief internal investigation and reinstatement of Jensen Huang do not erase the national security threats and questions that must be addressed," Reps. John Moolenaar, Rick Crawford and Brian Mast said in a joint statement.

Their inquiry may also examine what Nvidia knew about the customers and resellers involved, as well as what controls the company had in place.

Other lawmakers have also sought to preserve the paper trail. Sens. Elizabeth Warren and Chris Coons had asked senior administration officials on Nov. 11 to retain records related to Nvidia exports of advanced AI chips to China and associated national security concerns.

Nvidia has promised to hire a chief compliance officer and establish a committee overseeing national security and export controls. The board's investigation into Huang's conduct also remains underway.

Josh Gilbert, eToro's lead analyst for APAC and the Middle East, said investors will be watching whether those new safeguards carry meaningful authority.

Gil Luria, head of technology research at D.A. Davidson, said Nvidia has a limited window to prepare for the possibility that a future president may be less supportive of Huang.

With Trump's term ending in two years, Luria argued that Nvidia needs a clearer plan for who could take over if Huang were forced to leave again.

Gilbert pointed to Nvidia's roughly 75% revenue-growth forecast for the next financial year as evidence of strong near-term demand. The longer-term question, he said, is whether customer orders and artificial intelligence investment can sustain that pace.

Luria told CNBC that concerns about the broader AI trade remain, including the cost of financing data centers, potential overbuilding by neocloud companies and obstacles to building new facilities.

Bernstein analyst Stacy Rasgon said worries about whether AI spending is achievable and sustainable were not caused solely by Huang's departure. UBS, meanwhile, flagged Nvidia's roughly $390 billion in supply commitments and the need to keep demand flowing through its ecosystem.

Gilbert said part of Monday's rally reflected investors recovering earlier losses rather than pricing in new growth. Bernstein restored its "Outperform" rating and kept its $200 price target, while UBS maintained its $220 target.

"For the share price, it comes down to whether Nvidia can keep delivering as it has done over recent years," said Gilbert, adding that Huang's return could ease some concerns about Nvidia's longer-term outlook.

"The real worry has been what happens beyond next year. Having Huang back at the helm goes a long way to easing those concerns, because he's the one who sits down with the biggest customers and gets those multi-year orders over the line," he said.

— CNBC's David Sucherman, Justin Papp and Eko Prasetyo contributed to the story

#2720. AI adoption grows in Asia, but measuring the gains remains tricky

Businesses across Asia-Pacific say their investments in artificial intelligence are paying off, with the technology helping boost productivity and revenue. But putting a number on those gains is proving far less straightforward.

Some 58% of businesses using AI said their investments had already surpassed their initial costs, yet just 12% were very confident in their ability to accurately measure their return on investment, according to research conducted by Strand Partners for Amazon Web Services.

The survey included 9,750 business leaders across 10 Asia-Pacific markets: Australia, Indonesia, Japan, Malaysia, New Zealand, the Philippines, Singapore, South Korea, Thailand and Vietnam.

The biggest shift over the past year has been the growing number of companies moving AI projects into production, Jaime Vallés, AWS vice president for Asia Pacific, Japan and China, told CNBC in an exclusive interview.

"From year over year, probably the biggest difference is you're seeing a lot of ideas put into production," Vallés said.

Despite the reported financial benefits, many businesses remain in the early stages of AI adoption.

The survey found that 64% of businesses using AI across Asia-Pacific and Japan still rely on general-purpose AI tools or ready-made solutions for specific tasks, while only 19% of businesses have a formal and comprehensive AI strategy.

Among those with such an AI strategy, 35% had reached the most advanced stage of adoption, compared with just 8% of businesses without one, according to the research.

Technology alone is not enough to turn AI investments into meaningful business outcomes, Vallés said. He pointed to what he called the "four Rs of readiness": technological, cultural, skills and personal readiness.

"Where we see more meaningful impact is when you see the leadership using AI, building a culture of using AI and using AI to transform and not automate," Vallés said.

Financial services is among the sectors seeing stronger AI adoption. Vallés pointed to Vietnam's Techcombank as an example, saying the bank is using AI to assess loan applications and expand access to credit for small businesses.

The technology has helped the bank to analyze more data and approve some loan applications that might otherwise have been rejected, he said.

Techcombank, one of Vietnam's largest commercial banks, serves more than 16.5 million retail and corporate customers. According to an AWS case study, its modernization of digital banking infrastructure using AWS improved application response times by up to 35% and cut the time needed to launch new product features by 30%.

AI adoption continued to expand across Asia-Pacific, with all 10 markets surveyed recording increases from the previous year.

Australia led the group with an adoption rate of 61%, followed by Singapore at 59% and South Korea at 58%.

Still, businesses face significant obstacles to scaling AI investments. A shortage of AI and digital skills remains a key barrier, cited by 48% of respondents as an obstacle to adoption or expansion.

Despite those challenges, Vallés expressed optimism about Asia-Pacific's role in AI innovation, pointing to the region's cultural diversity and range of perspectives.

"I am convinced that most of the innovation in the fullness of time will come from our region," he said.

Vallés added that Asia-Pacific has the ingredients to become a global center for innovation, citing its diversity, talent, startup ecosystem and technology.

"APAC, to make it simpler, has all the ingredients to be in my view the core or the center of worldwide innovation," he said.

Governments across Asia also play a role in supporting Al adoption and addressing barriers to its wider use.

About 72% of government respondents said Al had increased productivity, according to a report released by the Pacific Economic Cooperation Council in November, which surveyed more than 500 experts across 23 Asia-Pacific economies.

Around 59% of respondents viewed government support as a moderately or very important factor in accelerating AI adoption next year, though existing regulations were also seen as a constraint, the report showed.

Such support is also taking share through regional cooperation. The Asian Development Bank and South Korea established a $20 million fund to help developing economies adopt Al and address gaps in digital skills and infrastructure, according to an announcement by the bank earlier this month.

#2718. Top Wall Street analysts favor these 3 dividend stocks for strong returns

Investors often favor dividend stocks for their regular income and the promise of potential share price appreciation. But selecting the right dividend stocks from a huge pool of companies can be difficult.

Top Wall Street analysts can help shortlist attractive dividend-paying stocks, as their recommendations are backed by thorough analysis of a company's financials.

Here are three dividend-paying stocks that are highlighted by Wall Street's top pros, as tracked by TipRanks, a platform that ranks analysts based on their past performance.

Devon Energy (DVN), an independent oil and natural gas exploration and production company, reported better-than-expected third-quarter results. The company announced a quarterly dividend of 32 cents per share for the fourth quarter, payable December 30. Based on the fixed dividends paid over the trailing 12 months, DVN yields 2.85%.

After hosting a virtual investor meeting with management, Goldman Sachs analyst Neil Mehta reiterated a buy rating on Devon, with a price target of $59. Mehta said that he is now more confident in management's outlook for delivering strong near-term capital returns as well as efforts to drive operational efficiencies and productivity gains.

Devon sees an opportunity to increase share repurchases, driven by solid free cash flow (FCF), contributions from the Eagle Ford divestiture and continued improvement in the balance sheet, Mehta said. Devon expects share buybacks worth more than $1 billion in Q4 2026, up from $414 million in the third quarter, backed by a strong pricing backdrop and improved capital efficiency.

Mehta, a 5-star analyst, said Devon expects about $6.3 billion of excess FCF in 2027, including about $3.5 billion of net proceeds from the Eagle Ford sale. That would enable the company to accelerate buybacks while progressing toward its goal of reducing gross debt to about $9 billion by early 2027. Mehta noted that under DVN's framework to return up to 70% of annual FCF, management favors share buybacks as it believes the stock is discounted compared to large-cap peers.

"Management expects continued capital efficiency gains from merger synergies, extended laterals, surfactant adoption and development of the newly acquired federal acreage," Mehta said.

Mehta ranks No. 876 among more than 12,500 analysts tracked by TipRanks. His ratings have been profitable 57% of the time, delivering an average return of 8.1%. See Devon Energy Stock Buybacks on TipRanks.

Energy Transfer (ET), is a limited partnership that operates about 140,000 miles of pipeline and associated energy infrastructure. The company recently announced third-quarter results showing net income per common unit growing about 18% year-over-year, to 33 cents. ET's cash distribution for Q3 2026 was 34.25 cents per common unit, up more than 3% year-over-year. At an annualized distribution of $1.37 per share, Energy Transfer yields more than 7%.

TD Cowen analyst Jason Gabelman reiterated a buy rating on Energy Transfer with a price target of $25. The company delivered better-than-expected Q3 results and announced the 1.2 billion cubic feet per day (bcf/d) South Mississippi Pipeline, a 215-mile, 36-inch natural gas line linking the Perryville Hub to the Florida Gas Transmission (FGT) network, Gabelman said. The project is supported by 20-year firm transportation agreements and is expected to enter service in 2030. ET expects to ultimately own 51% of the $1.6 billion project.

The 5-star analyst said that Energy Transfer lowered its 2026 capital spending outlook to $5.5 billion (midpoint of the guidance range) from $5.75 billion due to project timing. Energy Transfer expects capex to rise to $7 billion in 2027, while Gabelman projects $5 billion in both 2028 and 2029, based on disclosed projects.

Gabelman also highlighted management's commentary about optimization initiatives contributing an incremental $800 million to 2026 earnings before interest, taxes, depreciation, and amortization, a larger benefit than in recent years. "A return to less commodity volatility should the [Middle East] war end could bring some relative downside, though this is offset by continued strong growth prospects," he said.

Gabelman ranks No. 397 among more than 12,500 analysts tracked by TipRanks. His ratings have been successful 64% of the time, delivering an average return of 15.4%. See Energy Transfer KPIs on TipRanks.

Oil and gas exploration and production company ConocoPhillips (COP) delivered better-than-expected Q3 performance. The company announced an 11% hike in its quarterly dividend to 93 cents per share, effective with the Q4 dividend payable on December 1. Based on the dividends paid over the trailing 12 months, COP yields about 2.6%.

Following the Q3 results, Wells Fargo analyst Sam Margolin repeated a buy rating on ConocoPhillips, citing strong potential for further dividend increases. Margolin also trimmed his price target to $185 from $189 to reflect timing effects and the WAHA forward curve, though he said that COP could still re-rate as its natural portfolio lifecycle provides stronger protection against inflationary pressures than peers. The Waha forward curve tracks future natural gas prices based on the West Texas Waha hub.

Margolin, another 5-star analyst, highlighted that ConocoPhillips' Q3 2026 adjusted earnings per share of $3.27 was well above Wells Fargo and the Street's estimates of $3.04 and $3.05, respectively. Earnings were driven by solid price realizations, partially offset by higher depletion, depreciation and amortization and purchased commodities.

Margolin also noted COP's dividend increase and said the accelerated buyback enabled by current commodity prices could further support long-term dividend growth. He sees the possibility of ConocoPhillips' dividend growth rate exceeding its current 6% cost of equity into the end of the decade. Margolin expects COP to sustain an 8% dividend compound annual growth rate through 2029 while repurchasing $6.4 billion of stock annually, and reducing net debt-to-capital to less than 9% by the end of 2028 based on the assumption of an average Brent crude oil price of $75 a barrel.

"The message on the 3Q call was similar to that of 2Q, reiterating the moderation in reinvestment rate and commitment to dividend growth through the Willow start-up and after," Margolin said.

Margolin ranks No. 189 among more than 12,500 analysts tracked by TipRanks. His ratings have been profitable 69% of the time, delivering an average return of 16.6%. See ConocoPhillips Ownership Structure on TipRanks.

#2717. Peterson: Here’s what Bessent’s about-face on Jensen Huang reveals about Trump and China

For a minute there, it seemed like someone in the Trump administration might actually be willing to stand up to China.

For about a week and a half, Treasury Secretary Scott Bessent was the face of an emerging harder line on Nvidia over the potential risks its co-founder Jensen Huang's affairs posed to national security. Bessent was giving voice to China hawks who had felt sidelined by the Trump administration's willingness to sell artificial intelligence chips to  China.

They see China's AI program as a major threat, and want to restrain its growth by cutting off the supply of advanced chips to China. The resignation of Huang, who pressed the Trump Administration to allow the exports, seemed to signal that the hawks had found new influence in Washington.

By this weekend, Bessent had softened his public resistance to Huang's reinstatement. That came just in time for Nvidia's board to reappoint Huang as CEO at President Donald Trump's urging.

Whether any real damage was done to national security isn't clear. But it is safe to say that the incoming Democratic majority in Congress won't take Trump at his word when he said on CNBC Monday that the Department of Justice "did a pretty good investigation" that exonerated Huang.

Congress will look deeply into the worries Bessent himself raised about Huang's acknowledged romantic relationship with a woman The New York Times and others have reported was a Chinese intelligence asset.

Nvidia's board said it had received assurances from top U.S. officials and had concluded his return wasn't a national security risk.

A top concern is whether China was able to use the affair to help divert Nvidia's high-end artificial intelligence chips toward helping its military. Bessent in a Nov. 13 interview with CNBC outlined the stakes.

"Any leakage is very frustrating because it hurts the — it hurts our great companies and in the AI race it gives the Chinese an advantage," he said.

Bessent said that his department and others had wanted to blacklist Megaspeed, the company previously run by Alice Huang, Jensen Huang's paramour. But "Commerce has the pen," Bessent said.

The Commerce Department is headed by Secretary Howard Lutnick, Bessent's rival.

Putting Huang back at Nvidia wasn't originally Bessent's preference. He said in a Fox interview on Nov. 15 that the board should "go outside the company" to find a new leader. But by this past weekend, Bessent had apparently gotten on board with Trump's wishes.

"Jensen is on the outside now," Bessent told CBS on Sunday, in trying to explain his apparent policy shift.

Commerce has since taken steps to tighten some restrictions meant to prevent U.S. chips from helping China advance in the AI race.

But there is bipartisan worry about the Nvidia episode. "I think we need to catalog the damage that's been done," Sen. Tom Cotton, R-Ark., told The Wall Street Journal. He said Lutnick had not responded to letters he sent saying the administration should be "taking the threats of communist China more seriously."

But Trump has clearly put an upper limit on how aggressively anyone in his administration can push back on China. The president said Monday that he was swayed by the pageantry in his visit to China last week.

Xi "met me at the plane, which is something they've never done, which was a sign," Trump said.

Trump said he felt like he needed to intervene to back Huang because the stock market was plummeting in the wake of Nvidia's troubles — a development he also blamed on Democrats' midterm victory.

"I watched our country lose $5 trillion because of a bad election," he said. "If I didn't get involved, you'd probably have a very sad Nvidia for a long time to come." Now, its stock performance will be "unbelievable."

Bessent on Sunday played down the market risks. The company "continues to be fantastically profitable, and there is near unlimited demand for their chips worldwide," he told CBS.

Lutnick has embraced Trump's transactional urges. He has backed deals in which the U.S. takes equity stakes in companies such as Intel, saying the taxpayer deserves some of the upside.

Lutnick in January said he had heard Huang pitch Trump on allowing Nvidia to sell its relatively more advanced H200 chips into China.

"There are strong — two sides. We shouldn't sell them any or we should sell them." Where does he stand? "Right in the middle," Lutnick told Bloomberg. Trump decided to go ahead with the deal.

Bessent has said he is "neither a hawk nor a dove" on China, and is simply trying to manage the ultra-sensitive relationship between the world's two biggest economies. But the upshot of the Huang affair seems to be that Bessent wasn't able to sway the president on a matter that seemed cut-and-dried just days ago.

Trump said he discussed whether Huang should return to Nvidia with Lutnick, Bessent and Vice President JD Vance. "Howard Lutnick was very good. Very, he's very professional," Trump said on CNBC Monday.

"I call them the opposites, Scott and Howard," Trump added, before declaring them "both good."

#2716. U.S. adds Megaspeed to trade blacklist over export control concerns amid Nvidia controversy

The U.S. has added Singapore-based Megaspeed and six other Southeast Asian companies to its trade blacklist, citing national security and foreign policy concerns.

The Department of Commerce in a Federal Register filing cited a "significant risk" of companies being or becoming involved in diverting advanced computing products to unauthorized end users or end uses.

The blacklisting comes as Jensen Huang returns to the helm of Nvidia following his departure amid revelations of extramarital affairs, including one with former Megaspeed executive Alice Huang, who is under investigation by U.S. authorities.

Megaspeed International is a Singapore-based cloud computing firm that operates data centers across Southeast Asia.

The restrictions apply to all items subject to U.S. Export Administration Regulations, with license applications to be reviewed under a "presumption of denial."

The blacklist also includes PT Megaspeed International and MSFusion Sdn. Bhd. in Malaysia, as well as Megaspeed (Thailand) Co., Ltd. The filing identified 7Road International as an alias used by both Megaspeed's Singapore and Thailand entities.

The Commerce Department initiated an investigation into Megaspeed in 2024 for potential export control violations after identifying suspicious activity through its monitoring of potential semiconductor diversion, according to a join statement issued with the Justice Department.

In May 2025, Commerce Department investigators requested records from Nvidia regarding direct or indirect sales to Megaspeed. Nvidia placed Megaspeed on its "no sell" list the following month, according to the statement.

U.S. authorities, working with Singaporean law enforcement officials, also conducted an on-site end-use check at Megaspeed in June 2025 and interviewed its then-CEO Alice Huang. The Justice Department subsequently took the lead in the criminal investigation into the company.

Commerce also added an Alibaba subsidiary in Singapore, Apex Enterprise Solutions, to a trade blacklist, Reuters reported.

Alibaba said it would pursue all legal avenues to protect its rights and reputation, adding that Apex had never diverted advanced computing chips subject to U.S. export restrictions, according to Reuters.

Meanwhile, Singapore Prime Minister Lawrence Wong said at a news conference in Canberra, Australia, on Tuesday that investigations into Megaspeed were ongoing and that discussions with American authorities were continuing.

"We do not want Singapore to be used and abused as a conduit by companies to circumvent America's export controls," Wong said.

#2715. Observations after Sixers' 5-game win streak ends with blowout loss to Blazers

Observations after Sixers' 5-game win streak ends with blowout loss to Blazers originally appeared on NBC Sports Philadelphia

The Sixers' five-game winning streak was snapped Monday night by the Trail Blazers.

With a 128-111 defeat at Xfinity Mobile Arena, the Sixers dropped their first game of the year against Western Conference opposition and fell to 14-4. Portland improved to 11-6.

LeBron James had a season-high 23 points and six rebounds. Tyrese Maxey scored 22 points and Jaylen Brown added 20.

Deni Avdija led the Blazers with 31 points and seven assists. Donovan Clingan posted 15 points and 18 rebounds.

The Sixers were missing Joel Embiid (illness). Ja Morant (left hamstring strain) and Shaedon Sharpe (right knee meniscus surgery) were among Portland's sidelined players.

Next on the Sixers' schedule is a Wednesday night game vs. the Heat. Here are observations on their blowout loss to the Blazers:

Jabari Walker, listed at 6-foot-7, defied conventional wisdom by winning the opening tip-off against the 7-2 Cllingan. Maxey then canned a three-pointer.

Brown scored the Sixers' next four points with a pair of free throws and a short jumper. VJ Edgecombe's layup gave the team a 9-2 lead. Brown and Dean Wade both returned Monday after sitting out the last two games with left hamstring injuries.

Avdija got hot in the first quarter. He scored 11 quick points, made his first four field goals and helped the Blazers avoid a serious early deficit.

The Sixers took a 32-30 edge with 9.3 seconds left in the first when Walker jammed in a put-back dunk. The former Portland second-round draft pick played 17 minutes in his second start of the season and continued to look deserving of a rotation spot. He posted 10 points and five rebounds.

Adem Bona was available again following an eight-game absence with a cervical spine sprain. However, Dominick Barlow checked in first to back up Walker.

Barlow had an outstanding start to his night. He scored a clever lefty push shot over Clingan and threw down a dunk in the first quarter. Barlow also made a highlight defensive play in his opening stint, defending Avdija in isolation and rejecting his layup attempt.

Bona then subbed in for Barlow with 3:23 left in the first quarter. He had a positive first possession, grabbing an offensive rebound and driving in for a dunk.

Wade later played at center, too. Barlow logged 16 minutes and had six points. Bona recorded four points and two boards in five minutes. Sixers head coach Nick Nurse ended up using a 10-man rotation and did not play rookie guard Labaron Philon Jr. until fourth-quarter garbage time.

Brown delivered tons of efficient offense for the Sixers in the first half and sure didn't appear rusty. His layup late in the second quarter tied the game at 58-all. The five-time All-Star forward scored 19 first-half points on 8-for-10 shooting.

Avdija exceeded Brown's production, hitting heat-check threes, leaners through contact and just about everything he put up regardless of the defender. He tallied 25 points in the first half and shot 9 for 12 from the field.

James reached 16 points when he capped a high-scoring half by nailing a corner three. The contest was even at 69 apiece heading into halftime.

Monday's game was James' first of the season with three or more made three-pointers. The four-time MVP went 4 for 5 beyond the arc. He'd gone 10 for 46 (21.7 percent) over his first 13 games and was due for a good long-range night.

James was whistled for his third foul early in the third quarter. Brown picked up his fourth with 10:15 to go in the period, forcing him to sit for an extended stretch. Wade entered and James remained aggressive on his way to a third straight 20-point outing.

Avdija stopped lighting up the Sixers in the third period, but the team still had another unimpressive defensive quarter. Portland posted 32 points in the third and built its lead to double figures in the opening minute of the fourth.

The game devolved into a blowout. An Avdija dunk put the Blazers' advantage at 22 points with 5:15 on the clock and the Sixers didn't have a miracle comeback in store.

Even with all their wins, there's no question the Sixers have been trending downward on defense over the past couple of weeks, allowing regular dribble penetration and plenty of comfortable threes.

They'll aim to raise their level on that side of the floor and expect Embiid's presence to be a major help once he's back in action.

#2714. Roob's Observations: Why on Earth is Johnny Mundt playing more than Eli Stowers?

Roob's Observations: Why on Earth is Johnny Mundt playing more than Eli Stowers? originally appeared on NBC Sports Philadelphia

When you've lost seven in a row, there are countless questions to answer. For this Eagles team, they include: Why is Johnny Mundt playing more than Eli Stowers? What should the Eagles do with their top-10 draft pick in April? How will they replace Jordan Davis's snaps? How lackluster has the Eagles' passing game been?

We'll do our best to answer these and a number of other pressing questions facing the hapless Eagles, who are 2-7 for only the third time since 1975.

1. Someone has to explain to me why Johnny Mundt is playing more than Eli Stowers. Mundt is a 10-year veteran who has four catches for 16 yards this year and has not been close to the blocker we all expected when the Eagles signed him. For literally zero value, Mundt has played a ridiculous 220 snaps, or 24 ½ per game. Why? He brings nothing to the table. Then there's Stowers, a promising rookie who is finally healthy after dealing with a lingering hamstring injury. The Eagles invested a 2nd-round pick on Stowers, yet he's buried on the bench. In the three games since he came back healthy, Mundt has played 37 snaps and Stowers has played 32. There is absolutely no reason on Earth a 31-year-old journeyman with no receiving ability and minimal blocking prowess should be playing more than a 23-year-old rookie 2nd-round pick. None. Even if Mundt was good, the Eagles are 2-7 and this is a time to see what the young guys can do and get them as many reps as possible. Stowers got a few targets late in the Washington game and caught three passes for 53 yards, including a 34-yarder. He had more yards on that one 34-yard catch than Mundt has all year. And do you know when Mundt's last 34-yard catch was? It was in 2020. Six years and four teams ago. You're 2-7. What are we doing here? Play the young guys. Especially ones who have shown some promise. I don't know if Stowers will ever be a legit NFL tight end. But I do know we're not going to find out if he's not playing.

2. This is amazing to me: From 1986 through 1997 – over parts of 12 seasons – the Eagles went 26-0 when they had a 100-yard rusher. Randall Cunningham ran for 110 yards in a 13-11 loss to the Lions at the Vet in November 1986 and the Eagles didn't lose again with a 100-yard rusher until September of 1997, when Ricky Watters ran for 106 yards in a 21-20 loss to the Cowboys at Texas Stadium. They also had a 10-game winning streak with a 100-yard rusher from 2002 through 2005, a 13-game winning streak with a 100-yard rusher from 2013 through 2016 and a 19-game winning streak from 2020 through 2024. In all, since 1987 they're 114-24 with a 100-yard rusher. They're 27-3 in their last 30 games with a 100-yard rusher. That's totally nuts. This year? They're 0-0. No 100-yard rushers since last year.

3. Two of the bigger decisions the Eagles will have this offseason are interlocked. What to do with Jalen Hurts and what to do with what will likely be a top-10 pick – and quite possibly a top-5 pick. Let's operate under the assumption that Hurts will be healthy by next season and able to play. And for the purpose of this conversation let's also  assume the Eagles figure out a way to sign him to an extension so he's not going into the final year of guaranteed money in 2027. Because where are you going to find a better quarterback than a guy who took the team to two Super Bowls, winning one, has won 68 percent of his career starts and reached the playoffs in every full season he's played? Might be tricky to determine his value coming off this lost season, but Howie is Howie. He'll figure it out. With those two assumptions, the question becomes what do the Eagles do with such a premium draft pick if they're not going to take a quarterback, and I think it comes down to edge rusher or offensive tackle. And you can make a pretty good case for edge, and this draft is loaded at edge, I would go offensive tackle. Unless the Eagles see Markel Bell as the long-term replacement for Lane Johnson, which I doubt will be the case, that's priority No. 1. This offensive line is just a train wreck right now and Roseman's biggest offseason assignment is going to be rebuilding it. That may start with the coach, but it's got to include personnel, and if you're picking high enough to land a Jordan Seaton from LSU or Trevor Goosby of Texas that's a heck of a place to start. There's also the Jaguars' 1st-round pick, but that will obviously be way later than the Eagles' own, probably late enough to be a best-player-available pick. The Eagles have such a tradition of elite offensive tackles, with Jon Runyan, Tra Thomas, Jason Peters, Lane Johnson and Jordan Mailata. Time to find the next guy in the lineage.

4. With Jordan Davis no longer here, one guy I want to see more of is Ty Robinson, last year's 4th-round pick. Ty had a very good second half of training camp after a slow start but has only played 61 snaps, or about 6 ½ per game. I want to see him play. I don't need to see more Byron Young. He's played 19 snaps per game and barely caused a ripple. I've seen more out of Robinson in about a third the snaps. Davis averaged 36 snaps per game, and Jalen Carter is already playing 84 percent of the snaps so he can't play more. But I'd expect Moro Ojomo – who's at 60 percent and 42 snaps per game – to add about 10 snaps a game and Young to add maybe five and then whoever is the fifth guy – practice squad call-up Leki Fotu or Gabe Hall – is going to play about 10 snaps a game. So that should leave maybe a dozen more snaps for Robinson. I like the player and want to see more of him.

5. Here's a stat that really illustrates how lifeless the Eagles' passing game has been. The Eagles are averaging 9.7 yards per completion, which would equal the lowest in franchise history over a full season. They also averaged 9.7 in 1998. The only other year they were under 10 is 1974, when they finished at 9.8. The Eagles are last in the NFL with just 14 pass plays of 20 yards or more. Only five of those in the second half. Their last 2nd-half completion of 20 yards to a WR was in Week 2, when Jalen Hurts had a 20-yarder to Dontayvion Wicks and a 23-yarder to Darius Cooper. Man, this offense is just brutal.

6. Quez Watkins caught a 26-yard pass from Daniel Jones Thursday night in the Colts' loss to the Texans, and I know people around here aren't particulary fond of Quez and I get it. His Super Bowl drop was inexcusable. His fumble after a 51-yard catch cost the Eagles a win against the Commanders. His refusal to go hard for the ball cost Gardner Minshew two interceptions in a game against the Cowboys. His last few years here were a disaster. I get all that. But here's the thing. I'm just a fan of guys who don't give up no matter how extreme the odds, and Watkins never gave up on his NFL dream despite not playing a snap in 2024 or 2025 or the first half of 2026 and getting released by four teams. This is a dude who was a 6th-round pick in 2020 and had a decent year in 2021 with 43-for-647 before his career began to spiral downhill. The Eagles even brought him back last year on the practice squad for a bit, and despite all the high-leverage mistakes I found that he had a great attitude when I chatted with him after he got here in September: "Yeah, the last couple years have just been very stressful for me," Watkins said at his locker. "But at the same time, I'm still pushing, still going and trying to get back to where I was. I definitely have a lot of football left. It's just a matter of when. When my time comes, when my time comes back around, I'll be ready to ball again." The 26-yarder Thursday night was his longest catch since a 30-yard touchdown from Jalen Hurts in a win over the Packers at the Linc in November 2022. Four years ago. Easy to dislike a guy like Quez Watkins, and I don't blame anyone who does. Me, I can't help admiring his determination and resilience. He's a survivor, and in the NFL that's not an easy thing to be.

7. Joe Milton had a pretty funny answer when asked about the nickname Bazooka Joe, which cleverly combines his first name with a metaphor for his massive arm. "I don't like it," he said. Why not? "Because bazookas only travel so far." What does he prefer? "Mr. Rocket."  You might remember that Randall Cunningham told the beat guys after the 1995 playoff loss in Dallas – his last game as an Eagle – that he nearly named his newborn son "Johnny Rocket." Randall put four names into a hat including "Randall Jr." and "Johnny Rocket," and his wife Felicity – fortunately – picked Randall Jr., born in Las Vegas the day before that 30-11 loss, went on to become a two-time NCAA Division 1 high jump champ for USC. And Randall didn't play again for two years, until Brian Billick and the Vikings revived his career.

8. Sixteen of the 27 all-time field goals of 55 yards or longer against the Eagles – 59 percent – have been made since Nick Sirianni became head coach. Kickers have made more field goals of 55 yards against the Eagles this year (five) than the first 77 years of the team's existence (four). From 1933 through 2017, opposing kickers were 6-for-23 against the Eagles from 55 yards and out (26 percent). Since 2018, they're 21-for-28 (75 percent). Ten of the 27 55-yarders against the Eagles have come since opening day last year.

9. How wild is it that the Patriots' leading receiver who's a former Eagle isn't A.J. Brown but Mack Hollins, still going strong in his 10th NFL season. Hollins, the Eagles' 4th-round pick in 2017, missed three games this year but has 23 catches for 293 yards in six games compared to Brown's 11-for-128 in four games. Hollins, who won a Super Bowl ring with the Eagles as a rookie, has really had a remarkable career with six teams, catching 231 passes for 2,912 yards and 18 touchdowns after a very slow first few years. He's 33 now and has the 4th-most catches ever by a WR after his 32nd birthday drafted by the Eagles behind Hall of Famers Harold Carmichael and Tommy McDonald plus DeSean Jackson. Good ol' Mack Hollins. Still at it.

10. When Kyle McCord started for the Dolphins against the Colts Sunday, he became only the fifth quarterback drafted by the Eagles in the sixth round or later to start an NFL regular-season game. The first was Dick Jamieson, the Eagles' 25th-round pick out of Bradley in 1959. He started only one game in the NFL, for the New York Titans in 1960 and won it, 27-3 over the Bills at the Polo Grounds. Bob Berry – the Eagles' 11th-round pick out of Oregon in 1964 – started 52 games with the Vikings and Falcons from 1966 through 1974. Koy Detmer, a 7th-round pick in 1997 out of Colorado, went 3-5 in eight career starts with the Eagles, and Tanner McKee, a 6th-round pick out of Stanford in 2024, is now 1-4 in five career starts. The Eagles have only drafted 22 quarterbacks in franchise history who have started an NFL game. Of the 49 quarterbacks who've started a game for the Eagles, only 14 were drafted by the Eagles.

#2713. CNBC's The China Connection newsletter: AI race spreads from robots to smartphones

Hi, this is Evelyn, writing to you from Beijing. Welcome to the latest edition of The China Connection — a snapshot of what I'm seeing and hearing from local businesses.

I'm just back from APEC in Shenzhen, where I saw the leaders of the U.S., China and Russia gather along with other member economies. Beneath the fanfare, what's changed on the tech front in what's often dubbed China's Silicon Valley?

It only took a few months for a humanoid robot to get better at picking up household items.

Even after a human knocked a box and toys away from their original spots, a LimX robot quickly located them and moved them to pre-set locations. While still far from perfect, the smoothness of the demo contrasted with the robot's more stilted movements I'd seen early this summer — and inability then to react to rapid changes in tasks.

When we caught up in Shenzhen last week, CFO Jesse Liu declined to comment on LimX's IPO plans. But he told me that his conversations around the APEC CEO Summit revealed an excitement around a new development for humanoids: growing demand for robot chips that can run smarter large language models locally, instead of on the cloud.

"A lot of chipmakers are now happy about the latest developments in LLMs because they think robotic OEMs will actually begin to integrate more storage, and reasoning, and compute, and inference infrastructure on the edge, or locally inside the robot body," Liu said, referring to original equipment manufacturers.

"They do see robots moving from hundreds of units to thousands of units, to tens of thousands of units, beginning to enter that kind of automotive quantity," he said, pointing to chip suppliers such as Horizon Robotics.

New AI models, particularly GPT-6 Astra, have shown not just improved reasoning, but better control of robots.

That puts more of the onus on hardware makers, for robots or other devices, to create practical applications. LimX said it's working with business partners in over 30 markets, including Switzerland where the robots serve as power plant inspectors in rural areas.

The key for powering AI's transition into the physical world is on-device chips and more powerful batteries. That means Nvidia's most expensive, cutting-edge chips won't be the only ones powering generative AI models.

So far, even in basic consumer applications such as smartphones, AI isn't transformative enough to be a top-three selling point for mass-market users, Thomas Bai, AI product expert at smartphone company Honor, told me in Shenzhen.

He expects that in the next few years, as smartphone chips are able to run more powerful AI models, the "super agent" tech can become better at completing tasks on its own, and anticipating a user's needs — such as ordering coffee.

Counterpoint on Nov. 19 forecast that in the retail industry, "edge AI" revenue will surge sixfold by 2032, with roughly half of revenue coming from retail robots that clean and check inventory — often with the help of smaller AI models that can run on locally embedded chips.

But more capable, on-device AI also requires more battery power, Honor's Bai pointed out.

Honor's X70 Android smartphone, which launched last summer, boasts a battery with 8,300 milliampere-hours for a total device weight of just under 200 grams. That compares with 5,000 mAH for the 214-gram flagship Samsung Galaxy S26 Ultra, and roughly 5,400 mAH for this year's 249-gram Apple iPhone 18 Pro Max, according to GSM Arena.

LimX's Oli humanoid comes with 9,500 mAH battery capacity, lasting the robot about 1.5 hours.

Such AI applications, however, remain sensitive to the memory-chip shortage weighing on the industry.

The X70 was the best-selling Android phone in the entry to mid-tier range, said Shenzhen-based Counterpoint analyst Shiwen Ma.

While Ma said penetration of smartphones with generative AI features is higher in China at 55% versus 38% overseas, the offerings are still not essential to users, while rising memory-chip costs make it harder for manufacturers to bring AI-powered devices to the mass market.

Geely-backed automotive tech company EcarX's CFO Dylan Jeng told me earlier this month he expects the memory price surge to persist through 2027, and even the first half of 2028.

The chipmaker that can narrow the gap for on-device AI needs could find its moment soon.

Bessent tells CNBC Nvidia chip export policy is unchanged

U.S. Treasury Secretary Scott Bessent spoke exclusively on CNBC's "Squawk on the Street." President Donald Trump on Friday said he and Chinese leader Xi Jinping discussed their shared affinity for former Nvidia CEO Jensen Huang during their talks in China this week.

How China is using summits to expand its global influence

For a second year in a row, Xi has brought Russian President Vladimir Putin together with another major world leader on mainland Chinese shores. But U.S.-China trade, Taiwan and tech tensions remain unresolved as Trump returned from Asia.

Putin scores 'massive win' at first trilateral meeting

The first-ever three-way summit is "arguably a diplomatic victory for Moscow," said Andrius Tursa, an advisor for Central and Eastern Europe and Russia at Teneo. The view from inside the APEC welcome banquet showed the leaders were in close proximity from the start of the summit.

Got a confidential news tip? We want to hear from you.

#2712. Barlow looks set for season debut with Sixers after long days, ‘miserable' weeks

Barlow looks set for season debut with Sixers after long days, 'miserable' weeks originally appeared on NBC Sports Philadelphia

Dominick Barlow is on the verge of his season debut with the Sixers.

The team listed Barlow as probable for its Saturday afternoon matchup with the Bucks at Xfinity Mobile Arena.

Barlow practiced Friday and has been making clear progress in his recent on-court work recovering from an injury formally classified as a "lower back stress reaction."

He was a reliable rotation fixture last season, playing 71 regular-season games and starting 59. Barlow posted 7.7 points, 4.8 rebounds, 1.2 assists and 0.9 steals per contest in 23.8 minutes per game. The Sixers then made a common-sense call to exercise his $3.4 million option for the 2026-27 season.

The 23-year-old described his back injury as "an overuse thing."

"I guess there was an area or maybe I started my (offseason) workouts too early," he said. "I don't know what it was fully in terms of what caused it. Once it happened, it happened. Nothing I can do about it besides try to correct it and get my body feeling as good as possible."

He called the past few weeks "miserable" as he rehabbed and waited on the green light by the Sixers' training staff.

"The days just felt longer," Barlow said. "Trying to be engaged mentally, which I think I've done a good job with, but the physical aspect wasn't there. I'm somebody that's prided himself on competing, being available. Not being able to do that was hard, but I'm excited to kind of put this past me."

With LeBron James and Jaylen Brown on the team now, Barlow won't start at forward when the Sixers are at full strength.

He's still a multi-dimensional frontcourt piece for Sixers head coach Nick Nurse who may very well get substantial opportunities at backup center.

"Just cutting, playing hard, being ready to make the open shot," Barlow said. "And then I don't think you can ever have too much defensive versatility. I think that's something I can bring, try to help us be more versatile."

When Barlow checks into his first game of the season, he'll appreciate the moment.

"You don't really realize what you have until it's not there (anymore)," he said. "I'm just excited to play. …. Just playing the first stint … that's going to be the main thing I'm looking forward to."

A Sixers official said the following players were all "unrestricted" at the team's light practice:

Dean Wade (left hamstring soreness/tightness)

Bona still needs to practice with contact before a return is on the table, according to the official. Saturday's game will mark his eighth consecutive absence.

Brown and Wade have both been ruled out for the 13-3 Sixers' meeting with the 1-15 Bucks. Maxey was listed as questionable Friday night.

#2711. Observations after Sixers beat Bucks to move to 3-0, Embiid posts triple-double

Observations after Sixers beat Bucks to move to 3-0, Embiid posts triple-double originally appeared on NBC Sports Philadelphia

The Sixers stayed unbeaten Saturday night at Xfinity Mobile Arena, earning a 127-116 win over the Bucks and improving to 3-0 on the season.

Joel Embiid had his 10th career regular-season triple-double, tallying 28 points, 11 rebounds and 10 assists.

On his 30th birthday, Jaylen Brown posted 27 points and five assists. Tyrese Maxey had 26 points. VJ Edgecombe scored 17 and grabbed 13 boards.

The Sixers' absences on the first night of a back-to-back included LeBron James (low back/sciatica management). Sixers head coach Nick Nurse said pregame that he did not know yet who would be available for his team's matchup with the Pistons on Sunday.

Here are observations on the Sixers' victory Saturday:

Dean Wade made his Sixers debut and started in James' place. He'd missed the first two games of the regular season with a left ankle sprain.

The 3-and-D forward scored the night's first points by a sinking a corner three-pointer on the opening possession.

Embiid also had a sharp start. He moved well, made good decisions and served as the main hub of his team's offense in the early going.

The Sixers' seven-time All-Star center has often enjoyed facing Myles Turner. He entered the night with an average of 29.4 points in 18 previous games against the Bucks big man.

Embiid did a little bit of everything in the first quarter, posting eight points, four assists and four rebounds over nine minutes. After snatching an offensive board, Embiid drained a fadeaway jumper to give the Sixers a 10-1 lead.

One of Embiid's assists was quite stylish. He found Edgecombe open beyond the arc with a behind-the-back bounce pass. Edgecombe knocked down the shot and wound up going 3 for 4 from three-point range in the first quarter.

The Sixers added rookie Labaron Philon Jr. to their rotation. Jabari Walker stayed in the mix, though he didn't enter until the 7:36 mark of the second quarter.

Philon played four minutes in his NBA debut and missed his lone shot attempt, a corner three. On defense, Philon allowed a couple of blow-bys off the dribble. He generally looked like he belonged on offense, albeit without any production in his brief run.

Maxey banked in a half-court heave at the first-quarter buzzer to put the Sixers up nine points. The Sixers couldn't carry forward any momentum, however, and began the second quarter poorly.

Adem Bona had another rough stint amid a subpar start to the season. After Anfernee Simons missed a transition layup try, Ryan Rollins' three lifted the Bucks into a 40-37 lead.

The dreadful stretch for the Sixers on both sides of the ball called to mind how they'd fallen into a 29-point deficit Thursday night vs. the Cavaliers before clawing out a comeback win.

The Sixers mustered just seven points in first six minutes of the second quarter and had many stagnant possessions that concluded with contested jumpers. The team's transition defense was dismal for much of the second period. Milwaukee had the game's first 19 fast-break points.

The Sixers regained the lead late in the second quarter. Kentavious Caldwell-Pope made two three-balls in the second and nabbed three first-half steals. Embiid assisted a go-ahead, and-one Walker layup.

Brown hit a three with 11.8 seconds left in the second quarter. Unsurprisingly, his variety of shotmaking skills were useful without James in the lineup.

Even when James or Embiid sit, the Sixers should have a simple advantage in terms of volume of stars over most opponents this year. None of Brown, Embiid or Maxey had the sort of off night that would have improved the Bucks' odds of a road win. They'd all passed the 20-point mark by the end of the third quarter.

Maxey notched another final-possession bucket, this time with a more conventional make. He canned a jumper from the right elbow with 1.7 seconds left in the third to extend the Sixers' lead to 97-87.

The Bucks made a late push in the fourth quarter. Kel'el Ware's put-back, and-one layup cut the Milwaukee's deficit to 117-114.

The Sixers ultimately handled business. Embiid reached his triple-double by assisting a second-chance Brown jumper and and Caldwell-Pope soon drilled a dagger corner three. Embiid even threw down a last-minute dunk for good measure.

For the second consecutive season, the Sixers have a 3-0 start. They got to four wins last year before dropping their first game.

#2710. 2 blockbuster trades Pacers must target to save season after Tyrese Haliburton injury ruins start

2 blockbuster trades Pacers must target to save season after Tyrese Haliburton injury ruins start appeared first on ClutchPoints. Add ClutchPoints as a Preferred Source by clicking here.

The Indiana Pacers entered the 2026-27 season with the hope that their nightmare 2025-26 campaign was nothing more than a one-off, as Tyrese Haliburton was set to return from his year-long absence after rupturing his Achilles in the biggest game of his life — Game 7 of the 2025 NBA Finals against the Oklahoma City Thunder.

Haliburton did look healthy to start the new campaign, averaging 21.8 points and 9.5 assists in four games. However, after playing nearly 35 minutes in a 110-102 loss to the Milwaukee Bucks back on the 26th of October, reports came out that the Pacers star had suffered a strained hamstring, sidelining him for weeks.

To this day, Haliburton remains out with this injury, and it's unclear as to when he'll be making his return to the hardwood. Even after earning a 125-120 victory over the injury-ravaged Thunder on Monday night, they are only 6-12 on the season — good for just 11th place in the Eastern Conference.

Watch sports LIVE with fuboTV (free trial)

There is a long way to go in the season, and as they showed during the 2024-25 season, they can turn things around when they get a clean bill of health as a team. They started that season with a 6-10 record through 16 games, but still finished with a 50-win campaign.

But considering the question marks regarding Haliburton's health, it may not be the worst idea for the Pacers to not just strengthen the team, but also insure it in the event of another injury to their superstar floor general.

Here are two blockbuster trade ideas the Pacers have to entertain to rescue themselves from a brutal start.

Pacers trade: TJ McConnell, Jarace Walker, Ben Sheppard, Jay Huff, 2027 IND first-round pick, 2028 IND first-round pick swap, 2030 IND first-round pick

Trey Murphy III is expected to command plenty of interest on the trade market yet again even as the New Orleans Pelicans remain stubborn with making their current core work. The problem for the Pelicans is that the whole league knows of their dysfunction, and amid the team's instability, some of their players' performances have dropped off dramatically — with Murphy suffering one of the biggest drop-offs on the team.

Murphy scored just nine points in 35 minutes of play in the Pelicans' overtime loss to the Phoenix Suns on Monday, and this has been a continuation of a worrying trend for the 26-year-old forward.

He's averaging just 15.1 points per game this season, down from 21.5 last season, and his shooting splits have gotten worse across the board. Moreover, he's missed eight games already for New Orleans this season due to a knee injury.

Murphy's decline in production stems from his confusing role in the Pelicans' disorganized offense. It almost seems like their offense runs on vibes, and Murphy has gotten lost in the shuffle as a result — averaging over four fewer shots per contest than he did last season.

A change of scenery appears to be necessary for Murphy, and it's not too difficult to imagine just how much he'll thrive in this Pacers team — especially when Haliburton is healthy. Murphy can create his own shot, but he thrives when others set the table for him, and that's exactly what Haliburton will do.

Nearly 77% of Murphy's made field-goals are assisted, and with a dynamic playmaker like Haliburton in charge of the Pacers' offense, he should receive a boost in production in Indianapolis should the Pacers manage to actually trade for him.

The problem is that the Pelicans will once again be demanding the world in trade talks involving the 26-year-old. They were reportedly looking for a package similar to what the Brooklyn Nets and Memphis Grizzlies received for Mikal Bridges and Desmond Bane, respectively.

But New Orleans is not in a position of leverage, and the Pacers would do well to acquire Murphy without giving up Aaron Nesmith, a playoff hero in 2025, and Obi Toppin, an important rotation player. TJ McConnell will be missed, but he's been injured for most of this season anyway, and the team has Braden Smith and Quenton Jackson as capable guards off the bench.

They did give up two first-round picks in a trade for Ivica Zubac, so they will be hesitant to give away more draft assets. But for a player like Murphy, forking over those picks could very well be worth the risk.

Pacers trade: TJ McConnell, Jarace Walker, Ben Sheppard, top-12 protected 2027 first-round pick

Offense has been hard to come by for the Pacers amid Haliburton's injury. Through 18 games, they rank 21st in offensive rating, scoring just 111.3 points per 100 possessions.

While it's easy for fans and analysts to urge the Pacers to make a blockbuster trade, they have to keep in mind that they're building a roster around Haliburton. This means that they simply cannot just trade players for the sake of doing so.

Some would put Aaron Nesmith, Obi Toppin, and even Andrew Nembhard on the trade machine. But these three players are essential to Indiana's identity as a fast-paced team that can wreak havoc on defense as well. McConnell is also important, but there's a chance that he may never be sustainably healthy again.

But as was mentioned earlier, the Pacers need more of a scoring punch, and the player they need to seek out on the trade market has to be a good fit alongside the team's other core pieces as well. To that end, someone like Naz Reid could be a good get.

Reid has struggled mightily with the Charlotte Hornets, as his shooting efficiency has tanked completely amid that team's offensive woes. He is averaging 17.7 points per contest, but he's also shooting just 38.2% from the field, which is suboptimal to say the least.

Playing alongside Haliburton should once again help out Reid, and he gives the Pacers a go-to-guy for when Haliburton and Siakam need to catch a breather. He could also be more of a paint-bound defender, as Toppin and Siakam have the mobility to cover the more perimeter-oriented matchups.

Having Reid should also give the Pacers added matchup flexibility. In teams that can exploit Zubac's lack of perimeter mobility, Reid could be the man they go to when they need a bruising interior presence on the floor all while maintaining respectable floor spacing.

If Indiana can get Reid for the low price of a heavily-protected first-round pick, they should be all over that trade in a heartbeat. It's likely that the Hornets end up keeping Reid, but if Charlotte keeps on losing games, they have to at least think about trading him away.

#2709. China has changed tactics but is still thwarting India's rise, a U.S. panel says

China is "actively working to suppress India's rise," even as the relationship between the two major Asian powers has thawed in the past year, according to a U.S. panel that sees an opening for greater outreach to India.

On Nov. 18, a U.S. China Economic and Security Review Commission report said Beijing is applying military, economic, maritime, and other forms of pressure to constrain India.

China has recalled engineers and withheld equipment to slow India's electronics manufacturing, slashed pharmaceutical input prices to undercut new Indian plants, and imposed export controls on battery technology to impede the development of India's clean energy industry, the report said.

It added that these tensions offer the U.S. an opportunity to counter growing Chinese influence by building closer ties with India across key sectors such as pharmaceutical and defense.

Ties have improved since a deadly border skirmish in 2020, but experts told CNBC that trust is still thin between Beijing and New Delhi. However, they added that the uneasy peace with China is unlikely to make India align automatically with the U.S. as an ally.

The Indian foreign and commerce ministries and the Chinese foreign ministry did not respond to emails seeking comments.

The thawing in relations between India and China came amid industrial demand, as well as the need for "a whole lot of posturing for the U.S.," said Arpit Chaturvedi, South Asia advisor at strategic advisory firm Teneo.

Over the past year, India faced tariff pressures and needed to signal to Washington that its options remain open, including that of renegotiating relations with China, he explained, but added that as the trade deal with the U.S. is "close enough now," there is no pressing need for that signaling.

Further improvement in the relationship is also limited by reports of renewed border tensions between the two sides and Beijing's scant reciprocation on easing access to technology and people movement, he said.

Last month, an Indian military official said that confrontations with Chinese soldiers were ongoing across several locations along the country's northeastern border, even as top leaders from the countries met with fanfare at the BRICS Summit in September.

Another flash point was when Beijing put export controls on tunnel boring machines last month. "Our relations with China remain good, and if there aren't any new hurdles, they can improve further," India's Commerce Minister Piyush Goyal reportedly said after China's move to tighten TBM exports.

On Monday, a Chinese Foreign Ministry spokesperson reportedly said Beijing is ready to work with India to facilitate visa arrangements for business personnel from both sides amid reports that fewer Indian businessmen got visas to attend an annual trade fair in Shanghai.

The Chinese "actively disrupt" areas where India is trying to make progress in terms of moving global supply chains, developing advanced industrial technologies such as EVs, batteries, electronic components, and pharmaceutical ingredients, Pramit Pal Chaudhuri, South Asia practice head at Eurasia Group, told CNBC.

He added China is increasingly focused on containing India economically rather than through military overtures.

India's manufacturing push still relies on Chinese inputs, from electronics components and solar materials to rare earth magnets and specialized machinery. Between April and October, India imported goods worth $96.6 billion from China, up more than 30% from a year ago, as per commerce ministry data released earlier this month.

Its major imports from China include electronic goods, machinery, chemicals, transport equipment, iron and steel, and pharmaceutical products.

While India needs inputs for its manufacturing ambitions from Beijing and China values access to Indian markets, trust between the two sides is "still thin," according to Reema Bhattacharya, head of Asia research at risk intelligence company Verisk Maplecroft, citing a large trade imbalance and overlapping interests in South Asia and the Indian Ocean as additional issues.

#2708. U.S. court orders to proceed with planned ammunition detonation in Iowa

A U.S. appeals court on Monday cleared the Army to proceed with a controlled detonation of nearly five tons of degraded explosives at an Iowa ammunition plant, overturning a bid by environmental groups to block an emergency federal permit for the blast.

The order clears the way for the Army's plan to destroy around 68 barrels of lead azide at the Iowa Army Ammunition Plant in Middletown, near the Iowa-Illinois border, on Wednesday.

A three-judge panel of the 8th U.S. Circuit Court of Appeals denied a request by environmental groups seeking to challenge an emergency permit from the U.S. Environmental Protection Agency authorizing the operation.

"We are mindful of the very real risks presented by the Army's planned detonation of the stores of degraded lead azide," the ruling said. "However, we must acknowledge the realities of the situation: the degraded explosives present a risk of an uncontrolled and unplanned explosion, which would occur without the benefit of the safety measures that the Permit puts in place."

The planned blast, originally scheduled for Nov. 20, has been delayed three times amid regional concerns over possible health and environmental effects and criticism that the government failed to adequately communicate the potential risks to local communities.

The detonation is expected to produce a dust plume containing lead particles, a toxic metal that can cause serious health effects, according to the Iowa Department of Health and Human Services. Breathing in lead-containing particles or coming into contact with lead dust deposited on surrounding surfaces could pose potential health risks, the agency said.

The U.S. Army has argued that the risk of the material detonating on its own has increased as temperatures fall, and that the explosive compound, stored for more than two decades, has degraded to the point that moving it is too dangerous.

President Donald Trump said in a social media post Monday that he had told the Army to hold off until the court weighed in. He added that the decision would be subject to Iowa's Democratic Gov.-elect Rob Sand, who takes office in January, warning that further delay could risk "a large, spontaneous explosion."

In an X post late Monday following the court ruling, Sand said the Trump administration "should make this decision based on the information they have from the people they brought in to do the job months ago and have been on the ground in Middletown for weeks."

"Everyday Iowans are entitled to know how Middletown came to this situation, and learn why the administration believes this is the best path forward," Sand said, urging the federal government to share information with the public as soon as possible.
